NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization
of Large Language Models

Hao Sun1, Kun Wang8‡, Yi Zeng1‡, Jun Xu1‡, Guoqi Li4 and Ji-Rong Wen1

Understanding how complex cognitive functions are organized within artificial systems is central to
interpreting large language models (LLMs) and relating them to biological cognition. Yet although LLMs
exhibit broad cognitive-like behaviours, it remains unclear whether their internal representations form
reproducible functional systems that explain behaviour, failure and links to human cognition. Here we
present NeuroCogMap, a cognitive neuroscience-inspired framework that organizes internal features
of LLMs into functional parcels and links them to interpretable functions, cognitive capabilities and a
cognitive hierarchy. These parcels form a stable and semantically coherent organization that is partly
conserved across models and functionally linked to model outputs. Within this organization, major LLM
failures, including hallucination, bias, refusal failure and sycophancy, correspond to distinct disruptions
in representational and behavioural-control systems, yielding internal signatures for mechanism-guided
detection and targeted intervention. Beyond model behaviour, NeuroCogMap improves prediction of hu-
man cortical responses during naturalistic language comprehension, with the strongest correspondence
in higher-order association cortex. At the cognitive level, its internal signatures expose latent strategies
that guide refinements of classical models of human decision-making. Together, these findings establish
NeuroCogMap as a system-level framework for mapping functional organization in artificial systems
and for relating this organization to human cortical function and cognitive behaviour.
Introduction
Large language models (LLMs) have intensified the reciprocal relationship between cognitive neuroscience and
artificial intelligence [1, 2, 3]. They exhibit broad cognitive-like behaviours while providing experimentally
accessible systems in which complex functions arise within a single trained architecture [4, 5]. Yet these
capacities emerge without explicit modular design, pre-specified cognitive architecture or task-specific decom-
position, leaving open a fundamental systems question: how is cognitive organization instantiated within
LLMs? Understanding this organization is essential for explaining how model capabilities arise, diagnosing the
internal basis of systematic failures, and establishing whether artificial cognitive structure can provide testable
insights into biological cognition.
Current approaches provide only partial access to this question. Behavioural evaluation reveals what models
can do, but not how distinct cognitive functions are implemented, coordinated or controlled internally [6, 7, 8].
Mechanistic studies have identified neurons, circuits and sparse features associated with particular computations,
whereas representation-level analyses have revealed broader semantic structure [9, 10, 11, 12, 13]. These
approaches nevertheless tend to operate either at a local mechanistic scale or at a broad representational scale.
What remains missing is a system-level account of how distributed components form coherent functional units,
interact across levels of abstraction, support higher-order capabilities and break down during pathological
behaviour.
A similar explanatory challenge long shaped cognitive neuroscience. Complex human cognition could not
be understood from behaviour alone, nor from isolated local responses, but instead required organizational

NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Figure 1 | NeuroCogMap: a cognitive neuroscience-inspired framework for mapping cognitive structure in large language models.
a, Conceptual motivation. Human cognitive processes and neural activations are shown alongside cognitive capabilities and internal
activations in LLMs, motivating a shared framework for analysing cognitive organization across biological and artificial systems. b,c,
Framework construction. NeuroCogMap begins by collecting a broad capability space and associated evaluation datasets from the
literature (b). Sparse model features are then extracted from LLMs and used to identify parcels that define a functional atlas, which is
linked to a cognitive atlas of functions and further organized into cognitive capabilities (c). d, Internal structure of NeuroCogMap.
NeuroCogMap provides a multi-level description of cognition inside LLMs, linking parcel-level features and the structural connectome to
the functional atlas, cognitive atlas, cognitive capabilities and the final cognitive hierarchy. Example parcel- and capability-level
annotations are shown. e–g, From LLM organization to human cognition. NeuroCogMap supports analysis of pathological model
behaviours and their attribution, detection and mitigation (e), establishes a neural-level link to human cortical function by linking LLM
parcels to human neural activity (f), and guides cognitively grounded refinement of classical cognitive models (g).
principles that linked distributed brain activity to functional specialization [14, 15]. Functional parcellation,
representational mapping, hierarchical abstraction and pathology-based inference together established the
human brain as a structured cognitive system composed of interacting subsystems [16, 15, 17, 18, 19, 20]. These
same principles suggest a path forward for LLMs, while the models themselves offer an experimental advantage
unavailable in biology: their internal states can be observed, perturbed and replicated at scale [21, 22]. This
creates an opportunity not only to interpret LLMs more deeply, but also to study cognitive organization itself
under conditions of systematic observation and intervention.
These considerations motivate a framework that treats LLM cognition as an organized internal system rather
than as a collection of isolated behaviours or mechanisms. Here we introduce NeuroCogMap, a cognitive
neuroscience-inspired framework for mapping cognitive structure within LLMs. NeuroCogMap begins by
identifying coherent internal parcels from sparse model features, analogous to functional parcellation in
the brain. It then links these parcels to an interpretable cognitive atlas of functions and capabilities, and
further organizes them into a hierarchical architecture spanning perception, representation, abstraction and
application (Fig. 1b,c). In this way, NeuroCogMap provides a multi-level description of cognition inside LLMs,
from localized functional units to higher-order cognitive organization (Fig. 1d).
We then asked whether NeuroCogMap could analyse systematic model failures, support comparison with
human cortical organization and guide cognitive-model discovery. First, pathology analyses showed that
representational and behavioural-control pathologies exhibited dissociable patterns of disruption across circuits,
parcels, capabilities and hierarchy, and that these pathology signatures enabled detection and mechanism-
guided mitigation (Fig. 1e). Second, human-alignment analyses showed that NeuroCogMap parcel activity
improved cortical-response prediction during naturalistic language comprehension relative to conventional
model representations, with the most strongly aligned parcels showing functional correspondence with higher-
order cortical systems in both functional profile and semantic description (Fig. 1f). Finally, cognitive-model
analyses identified latent strategy signatures in LLM-based behavioural simulations and used them to guide
refinements of classical decision-making models that improved held-out behavioural fit (Fig. 1g). Together,
these results establish internal cognitive organization as an explanatory level connecting model mechanisms to
capabilities and pathology, while providing a common basis for comparison with human neural and behavioural
data without implying biological equivalence.
Results
NeuroCogMap provides a multi-level map of cognitive organization in LLMs
Framework overview.
To characterize how cognitive functions are internally organized in LLMs, we developed
NeuroCogMap, a multi-level framework inspired by cognitive neuroscience. NeuroCogMap links a functional
atlas of parcels with a structural connectome among them, a cognitive atlas assigning interpretable functions
to those parcels, cognitive capabilities linked to weighted sets of parcels and a cognitive hierarchy spanning
perception, representation, abstraction and application (Fig. 1d). We constructed the framework by curating
a capability space and associated evaluation datasets, extracting sparse autoencoder (SAE) activations and
clustering features according to their task-evoked response profiles [10, 23, 15]. We then estimated directed
cross-layer structural connections among parcels, assigned parcel-level cognitive functions and linked parcels to
capabilities using task-evoked activation, intervention effects and semantic consistency. Unless otherwise stated,
analyses used models spanning parameter scales and training stages, including Gemma2-2B, Gemma2-9B-IT
and Llama-3.1-8B [23, 24, 25]; implementation details are provided in Methods.

candidate atlas granularities (𝑘= 10–300), using a combined score of clustering quality, functional-description quality and functional

## Page 5


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Functional parcels emerge as stable and interpretable cognitive units in LLMs
A central question for NeuroCogMap is whether internal LLM activity forms functionally meaningful units
rather than arbitrary clusters of sparse features. We evaluated parcel organization along three criteria:
stable granularity, internal coherence and cross-model consistency. Across candidate atlas granularities,
a joint score combining clustering quality, functional-description quality and functional non-redundancy
peaked at 270 parcels (score = 0.771; Fig. 2a,a1; Supplementary Table 2), indicating an intermediate-
scale organization. Parcels were also internally coherent: semantic similarity among top-activating texts
was consistently higher within than between parcels (mean intra-parcel similarity, 0.638; mean inter-parcel
similarity, 0.433; 𝑊= 36585.0, 𝑃< 0.0001; Fig. 2a,a2; Supplementary Table 2). Finally, matched parcels
showed substantially higher activation-pattern correspondence than baseline directions across Gemma2-2B,
Gemma2-9B-IT and Llama-3.1-8B comparisons (Fig. 2a,a3), indicating that parcel-level organization is partially
shared across models.
Parcels were next evaluated for whether they encoded interpretable cognitive functions and whether such
functions generalized across models. Functional descriptions generated from parcel activation profiles predicted
parcel activation rankings on held-out examples. NeuroCogMap achieved higher prediction accuracy than
random clustering, neuron-based and keyword-only baselines (two-sided paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with
Holm correction, 𝑛= 40; 𝑊+ = 673–773, 𝑍= 3.54–4.88, all 𝑃Holm ≤0.001; Fig. 2b,b1; Supplementary Table 2),
indicating that atlas-derived descriptions captured predictive functional structure rather than plausible verbal
summaries alone. Targeted intervention on parcel activations shifted model outputs in directions predicted
by assigned parcel functions. Larger intervention success rates were associated with larger output changes
(Fig. 2b,b2), showing that the targeted parcels made functionally consistent contributions to model outputs
under perturbation. Parcel-level cognitive functions were also partially reproducible across models: matched
parcels were substantially more likely than random baselines to exhibit identical or partially similar cognitive
meanings across LLMs (Fig. 2b,b3). Together with the cross-model activation correspondence above, these
results suggest that NeuroCogMap captures a partially shared functional vocabulary across models at the level
of both activation dynamics and cognitive interpretation.
Parcels organize into cognitive capabilities and hierarchical layers.
Having established parcels as stable and interpretable functional units, we next examined whether they formed
selective links with higher-order cognitive capabilities. Parcels most strongly linked to a given capability showed
higher activation than unrelated parcels across top-ranked parcel–capability pairs (paired 𝑡-tests,𝑡45 = 2.75–
4.02, all 𝑃≤0.01; Fig. 2c,c1,left). The same selectivity was observed in the reciprocal analysis, where
top-linked capabilities for a fixed parcel were more strongly activated than unrelated capabilities (paired 𝑡-tests,
𝑡40 = 5.43–8.82, all 𝑃< 0.0001; Fig. 2c,c1,right). We next tested whether these links were causally meaningful.
Suppressing strongly linked parcels or capabilities produced significantly larger decreases in ground-truth log
probability than suppressing unrelated units across both parcel-to-capability and reverse-direction perturbations
(paired 𝑡-tests, 𝑡34 = 2.75–3.92 and 𝑡39 = 2.76–3.46, all 𝑃< 0.01; Fig. 2c,c2; Supplementary Table 3).
We next examined whether these capabilities formed a meaningful hierarchy. Guided by Bloom-inspired
levels of cognitive complexity [19], we organized cognitive capabilities into four operational layers—perception,
representation, abstraction and application (Defined in Methods and Supplementary Table 7) and tested
whether this stratification was reflected in parcel structure. Parcels assigned to a given layer were consistently
more semantically similar to their own layer description than to descriptions of other layers (Fig. 2d,d1). This
pattern was observed across perception, representation and abstraction layers (within-layer similarities, 0.541,
0.503 and 0.452, respectively; two-sided paired 𝑡-tests, 𝑡19 = 3.74–7.83, all 𝑃< 0.001), while the application
layer showed the same overall trend with significant separation from representation and abstraction layers
(𝑃= 0.017 and 𝑃= 0.022; Fig. 2d,d1), supporting the semantic coherence of the proposed hierarchy.
Critically, this hierarchy was also supported by evidence for operational prerequisite structure rather than
descriptive grouping alone. Controlled synthetic benchmarks were designed to test whether lower-order
retrieval and knowledge-recall support facilitated higher-order reasoning across the perception-to-abstraction
(L1→L3) and representation-to-abstraction (L2→L3) contrasts while holding architecture and optimization
fixed. To ensure a fair comparison with matched training-example counts, Fig. 2d,d2 used Double L3 and
Treble L3 as volume-matched L3-only baselines, constructed with twofold or threefold L3 training data to
match the corresponding multi-level conditions. In the in-context regime, adding lower-level retrieval support
5


## Page 6


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
improved two-hop reasoning relative to higher-level reasoning training alone (Welch’s two-sample 𝑡-tests,
𝑛= 5 runs per condition, 𝑡= 5.90–26.17, all 𝑃< 0.001; Fig. 2d,d2). Adding lower-level knowledge-recall
support similarly improved parametric reasoning (𝑡= 6.67–9.08, all 𝑃≤0.01; Fig. 2d,d2). In mixed reasoning
tasks requiring both contextual and parametric routes, jointly providing both lower-level supports yielded
the strongest performance across model scales (𝑡= 14.60–42.80, all 𝑃< 0.001; Fig. 2d,d2). Additional
semantic-dispersion and structural-degree analyses of hierarchy-associated parcels are reported in Extended
Data Fig. 11, providing convergent support for the proposed hierarchy by showing that higher-level capabilities
recruit more semantically diverse parcels, whereas lower-level parcels occupy more connected positions within
the model-level structural connectome.
Taken together, these results show that NeuroCogMap does not merely identify isolated functional parcels.
Rather, it organizes them into a structured cognitive system in which parcels map selectively and causally onto
capabilities, and capabilities in turn form a semantically coherent and functionally dependent hierarchy. This
higher-order organization provides the basis for the pathology, brain-alignment and cognitive-model analyses
that follow.
NeuroCogMap reveals representational pathologies in LLMs
Functional brain maps, lesion studies and pathology-based inference have long been used in neuroscience to
relate behavioural symptoms to disruptions of distributed cognitive systems [16, 15, 17, 18, 20]. Motivated by
this methodological logic, we asked whether an analogous cognitive map of LLMs could make model pathologies
amenable to systematic analysis: revealing the internal systems associated with pathological generation, testing
whether these signatures detect pathological outputs, and using them to guide targeted mitigation.
To characterize the internal organization underlying pathological behaviour, we compared normative and
pathological responses across four levels of NeuroCogMap: circuit connectivity, parcel activation, capability
recruitment and hierarchical organization (Fig. 3a). Circuit connectivity refers to task-evoked coupling
among functional parcels, estimated from answer-token trajectories and interpreted within the model-level
structural connectome. This structural constraint is important because functional coupling alone does not
establish directed influence. This contrastive framework allowed us to identify whether pathology emerged
from disrupted coordination among structurally linked functional units, abnormal parcel recruitment, altered
capability engagement or shifts across cognitive layers (See Methods).
We first examined representational pathologies, in which factual or social representations are incorrectly
selected, weighted or coordinated during generation. Here we focus on hallucination, with social-bias analyses
providing a parallel representational-related case in Extended Data Fig. 8. By contrast, behavioural-control
pathologies, including refusal failure and sycophancy, primarily involve failures to regulate outputs under
adversarial instruction or social pressure; this distinction was consistent with aggregate activation differences
between representation-related and behavioural-control-related systems (Extended Data Fig. 7).
Hallucination arises through distinct representational routes
We first asked whether hallucination reflects a single internal failure mode or multiple forms of representational
pathology. To this end, we compared truthful and hallucinated responses on two complementary datasets:
TruthfulQA, which contains questions designed to elicit common misconceptions [27], and NQ-Open, which
consists of direct factual queries without misleading premises [28]. Dataset details and representative task
patterns are summarized in Supplementary Table 1 and the Supplementary Information. NeuroCogMap
revealed a marked dissociation between these settings. Hallucinations in TruthfulQA primarily reflected a
failure of higher-order evaluative control over misleading representations, whereas hallucinations in NQ-Open
reflected a failure to coordinate otherwise relevant factual retrieval systems into a coherent answer-grounding
pathway (Fig. 3b–e).
In TruthfulQA, hallucination reflected impaired evaluative control over misleading representations rather
than absent factual retrieval. Truthful responses coupled retrieval to monitoring pathways, including Veracity-
Check Module–Affirmation Detector coupling (Δ𝑤= 0.57, 𝑃= 0.0087), whereas hallucinated responses strength-
ened local retrieval circuits such as Geographic Retrieval–Country Identification (Δ𝑤= −0.68, 𝑃= 0.026; Fig. 3b).
Parcel activity showed the same shift: truthful responses preferentially recruited evaluative parcels, including
Binary Fact-Check (Δ𝑎= 47.82, 𝑃= 0.0068) and Outcome Detection (Δ𝑎= 21.10, 𝑃= 0.038), whereas halluci-

## Page 8


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
nated responses over-recruited encyclopaedic lookup parcels, including Country Identification (Δ𝑎= −39.28,
𝑃= 0.0052) and Celebrity Knowledge Retrieval (Δ𝑎= −29.72, 𝑃= 1.9×10−9; Fig. 3c). The capability profile and
hierarchy showed the same pattern: truthful responses preferentially engaged Causal reasoning (Δ𝑎= 15.50,
𝑃= 2.0 × 10−5), Verification (Δ𝑎= 11.80, 𝑃= 2.7 × 10−4) and Judgment (Δ𝑎= 9.96, 𝑃= 4.5 × 10−5),
together with higher-order abstraction and application layers, whereas hallucinated responses shifted towards
retrieval-dominated generation and lower perception and representation layers (Fig. 3d,e). Thus, TruthfulQA
hallucination was associated with locally reinforced associative retrieval that was insufficiently constrained by
higher-order evaluative control.
NQ-Open hallucinations followed a distinct representational pattern, reflecting fragmentation of factual-
retrieval systems during direct factual answering. Truthful responses depended on coordinated factual-
access circuits, including stronger Internal Geopolitical-Trivia Retrieval–Real-Name and Role Retrieval coupling
(Δ𝑤= 0.47, 𝑃= 0.0017) and Geographic Knowledge Retrieval–Fact-Retrieval Core coupling (Δ𝑤= 0.44,
𝑃= 0.0051). Hallucinated responses retained retrieval activity but showed fragmented factual coordination,
including weakened Internal Geopolitical-Trivia Retrieval–Wikipedia Fact Retrieval coupling (Δ𝑤= −0.74,
𝑃= 2.0 × 10−6; Fig. 3b). Parcel activity showed the same dissociation: truthful responses more strongly
recruited Country Identification (Δ𝑎= 39.66, 𝑃= 2.0 × 10−5), Geographic Retrieval (Δ𝑎= 22.55, 𝑃= 2.9 × 10−4),
Entity Recall (Δ𝑎= 13.12, 𝑃= 4.9 × 10−6) and Fact-Retrieval Core (Δ𝑎= 11.86, 𝑃= 1.1 × 10−5), whereas
hallucinated responses disproportionately recruited Real-Name and Role Retrieval (Δ𝑎= −22.11, 𝑃= 1.4×10−10;
Fig. 3c). The capability profile and hierarchy further indicated disruption of a distributed factual-answering
regime. Truthful responses jointly engaged Context understanding (Δ𝑎= 7.66, 𝑃= 3.7 × 10−4), Knowledge
learning (Δ𝑎= 5.32, 𝑃= 1.4 × 10−21), Verification (Δ𝑎= 3.63, 𝑃= 2.0 × 10−8) and Information retrieval
(Δ𝑎= 3.28, 𝑃= 0.0086), with broad engagement across all four hierarchy layers. Hallucinated responses
instead disrupted this cross-layer coordination (Fig. 3d,e). Thus, NQ-Open hallucination reflected not selective
loss of retrieval, but failure to coordinate factual access, contextual understanding, verification and answer
generation into a coherent response pathway.
Together, these results show that hallucination is not mechanistically uniform. TruthfulQA hallucinations
reflected insufficient higher-order monitoring of misleading premises and retrieved associations, whereas
NQ-Open hallucinations reflected disrupted coordination among factual-retrieval modules. NeuroCogMap
therefore characterizes hallucination not as a generic generation error, but as a systems-level disruption in how
knowledge is retrieved, evaluated and coordinated during generation.
Hallucination signatures support detection and targeted intervention
We next asked whether the pathology signatures identified above were merely descriptive or could support
prediction and intervention. We therefore constructed a hallucination detector from NeuroCogMap-derived
signatures spanning parcel recruitment, capability engagement and circuit organization. Responses were scored
according to their alignment with truthful and hallucinatory pathology signatures (see Methods).
Across both model sizes, NeuroCogMap outperformed uncertainty-based, self-consistency-based and
representation-probing baselines (Fig. 3f; see Supplementary Information for baseline definitions). Across HaluE-
val [29], MedHallu [30], Dolly closed-QA [31], NQ-Open [28], SciQ [32] and TruthfulQA [27], NeuroCogMap
achieved mean AUROCs of 0.681 in Gemma2-2B and 0.840 in Gemma2-9B-IT, exceeding SelfCheckGPT [33],
hidden probing [34] and uncertainty-based methods [35, 36, 37]. These results indicate that hallucination was
more accurately detected from structured pathology signatures than from token-level uncertainty or generic
hidden-state separability.
We then converted the same pathology map into an intervention target. Enhancing parcels under-recruited
in truthful responses and suppressing parcels over-recruited in hallucinatory responses improved factual
accuracy across MedHallu [30], NQ-Open [28] and TruthfulQA [27] in both model sizes, with the largest
gains in settings characterized by impaired evaluative control or disrupted factual coordination (Fig. 3g).
Additional analyses in Llama-3.1-8B further supported the cross-model generality of these pathology signatures
for detection and intervention beyond the Gemma2 family (Extended Data Fig. 10).

values indicate stronger activation or connectivity in successful-refusal responses, whereas negative values indicate stronger activation or

Dataset details and sample sizes are provided in Methods and Supplementary Table 1; intervention procedures are described in Methods.
NeuroCogMap reveals behavioural-control pathologies in LLMs
We next examined behavioural-control pathologies, in which represented content is incorrectly inhibited,
executed or socially modulated during generation. Here we focus on refusal failure, with sycophancy analyses
providing a parallel behavioural-control case in Extended Data Fig. 9. Consistently, both behaviours showed
larger pathology–normal activation differences in behavioural-control-related parcels and capabilities than
in representation-related systems (Extended Data Fig. 7). This suggests that behavioural-control pathologies
arise from abnormal modulation of control systems rather than primary disruption of belief representations.
Refusal failure reflects a shift from control to procedural execution
We first examined refusal failure as a prototypical behavioural-control pathology by comparing successful-refusal
and refusal-failure responses on AdvBench [38]. NeuroCogMap revealed a shift from monitoring–evaluation–
negation control during successful refusal to planning and procedural execution during refusal failure, in which
refusal-failure responses were associated with stronger activation of actionable response patterns (Fig. 4a–d).
Successful refusal engaged control circuits linking risk detection, negation and answer gating, with represen-
tative increases in Negative-Motivation Detector–Negation and Self-Reference coupling (Δ𝑤= 1.21, 𝑃< 10−300),
whereas refusal failure strengthened execution-oriented pathways such as Idea and Plan Detection–Procedural
Guidance coupling (Δ𝑤= −0.36, 𝑃= 4.5 × 10−38; Fig. 4a). Parcel activity showed the same transition: suc-
cessful refusal preferentially recruited Legal-Moral Evaluation and the Privacy and Refusal Engine, including
a large increase in Legal-Moral Evaluation (Δ𝑎= 105.48), whereas refusal failure activated Illicit Behaviour
Processing and Concrete Action Processing (Fig. 4b). At the capability level, successful refusal engaged Ethical
reasoning, Alignment and Safety, whereas refusal failure shifted towards adaptive generation capabilities,
including Zero-shot learning and Controllable generation (Fig. 4c). This dissociation was concentrated in the
Situated Application and Social Interaction layer, indicating that refusal failure arose primarily from a shift
in behavioural control rather than from failure to parse or represent harmful content (Fig. 4d). Together,
9


## Page 10


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
these results characterize refusal failure as a behavioural-control pathology in which harmful instructions are
rerouted from safety-related response selection towards procedural action generation.
Refusal-failure signatures support detection and targeted intervention
We next asked whether the refusal-failure signatures identified above could support prediction and intervention.
Using NeuroCogMap-derived signatures spanning circuit, parcel and capability levels, we constructed a detector
to distinguish successful-refusal from refusal-failure responses and used the same pathology map to guide
parcel-level intervention (Fig. 4e,f).
Across AdvBench [38] and JBB-Behaviors [39], NeuroCogMap achieved near-ceiling refusal-failure detection
in both Gemma2-2B and Gemma2-9B-IT (mean AUROC, 0.992 and 0.990), outperforming perplexity-, logits-
and perturbation-based baselines [40, 41, 42] (Fig. 4e; see Supplementary Information for baseline definitions).
Thus, refusal failure was better detected as a structured breakdown in behavioural-control organization than
as a generic change in token surprise, output logits or perturbation sensitivity.
NeuroCogMap-guided steering substantially improved refusal accuracy when residual control pathology
remained, including a large gain on AdvBench [38] in Gemma2-2B (38.3% to 98.6%), while effects were
constrained when baseline refusal was already near ceiling (Fig. 4f). Additional analyses in Llama-3.1-8B [25]
further supported the cross-model generality of these pathology signatures for detection and intervention
beyond the Gemma2 family (Extended Data Fig. 10).
NeuroCogMap reveals convergent functional organization in LLMs and human cortex
Having shown that NeuroCogMap-derived organization characterizes and modulates LLM failure modes, we
next asked whether the same functional organization also relates to external neural measurements. Recent
studies have shown that LLM activations can predict human brain responses during naturalistic language
comprehension, suggesting that LLMs may provide computationally explicit models of cortical language
processing [43, 44, 45, 46]. However, prediction from generic hidden states does not establish whether
this correspondence reflects an organized functional structure. We therefore asked whether the organization
revealed by NeuroCogMap corresponds to human cortical function at the levels of parcel activity, cortical systems
and representational geometry. Specifically, we tested whether NeuroCogMap parcels predict cortical responses
better and whether the resulting parcel–cortex alignments preserve cognitive-functional and representational
structure within cortical systems.
NeuroCogMap predicts cortical responses in higher-order networks
We first evaluated this correspondence using encoding models of naturalistic language comprehension. Using the
LeBel story-listening fMRI dataset, in which participants listened to extended autobiographical narratives [47],
we mapped cortical blood-oxygen-level-dependent (BOLD) responses, an indirect haemodynamic measure of
neural activity, onto the 100-parcel Schaefer cortical atlas and grouped the resulting parcel-level responses
according to the Yeo seven-network organization for system-level analyses [15, 14]. NeuroCogMap parcel
activations of LLMs were then entered jointly into ridge-regression encoding models to predict held-out cortical
BOLD responses (See Methods).
NeuroCogMap achieved the highest prediction accuracy among all compared representations (mean 𝑟=
0.407; Fig. 5a, left). It outperformed lexical and embedding-based baselines, including WordRate [17]
(𝑟= 0.123), Word2Vec [48] (𝑟= 0.158) and BERT [44, 49] (𝑟= 0.219), as well as LLM-feature baselines,
including Language Context [45] (𝑟= 0.361) and Language Standard [50, 46] (𝑟= 0.367). All comparisons
against NeuroCogMap were significant after Benjamini–Hochberg correction [51]. Thus, structured parcel-level
activity captured cortical variance beyond conventional embeddings and generic LLM features.
The spatial distribution of this improvement localized the NeuroCogMap advantage to higher-order as-
sociation cortex (Fig. 5a, right). Relative to Language Standard, the largest gains were concentrated in
language-relevant Default network regions, including left parietal language-comprehension (Δ𝑟= 0.268), left
prefrontal semantic-retrieval (Δ𝑟= 0.174) and left temporal language parcels (Δ𝑟= 0.174), with additional
gains in right temporal Default regions (Δ𝑟= 0.190 and Δ𝑟= 0.113). This localization suggests sensitivity to
the semantic retrieval and contextual integration demands of narrative comprehension.

## Page 12


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Aggregating prediction accuracy within Yeo 7 functional networks confirmed this systems-level localization
(Fig. 5b). Accuracy was highest in Default, Frontoparietal Control and Salience/Ventral Attention networks, and
lower in primary sensory systems, consistent with the semantic integration and control demands of naturalistic
story comprehension.
NeuroCogMap captures semantic and representational correspondence with human cortical function
We next tested whether neural predictability was accompanied by cognitive-functional correspondence. Human
cortical parcels were assigned Neurosynth-derived functional profiles across Cognitive Atlas terms [52], and
NeuroCogMap parcels were ranked for each cortical parcel by prediction accuracy. We then compared the
functional descriptions of top- and bottom-ranked LLM parcels with the corresponding cortical profile using
text-embedding cosine similarity [53].
Across most cortical systems, top-ranked NeuroCogMap parcels showed higher semantic similarity to human
parcel functions than bottom-ranked parcels (Fig. 5c). This separation was strongest in higher-order systems,
including Dorsal Attention, Salience/Ventral Attention, Frontoparietal Control and Default networks, and
weaker in Somatomotor and Limbic systems; LLM-judge evaluation showed the same pattern. Thus, neural
predictability was systematically coupled with cognitive-functional agreement: the LLM parcels that best
predicted a cortical parcel also tended to express functions closer to its meta-analytic cognitive profile.
This correspondence was also evident in individual narratives. In the held-out story Where There’s Smoke,
representative human–LLM matches formed an interpretable systems-level pattern spanning attention, memory,
control and social-affective processing (Fig. 5d). For example, episodic memory retrieval in the default-mode
system aligned with an entity-recall module (rank = 1, PCC = 0.455, similarity = 0.640). Thus, human–LLM
correspondence extended beyond isolated parcel matches to coordinated functional systems recruited during
naturalistic narrative understanding.
Finally, we tested whether this correspondence extended from parcel-wise matching to within-network
representational geometry using description-based Representational Similarity Analysis (RSA) [54].
In
Salience/Ventral Attention, Frontoparietal Control and Default networks, we compared Neurosynth-derived
human parcel similarity matrices with NeuroCogMap similarity matrices constructed from the matched LLM
parcel descriptions. This analysis revealed a structured but selective hemispheric pattern (Fig. 5e): alignment
was significant in the left Default network (𝑟= 0.225, 𝑃= 0.047) and strongest in the right Frontopari-
etal Control network (𝑟= 0.774, 𝑃= 3.1 × 10−8), with a positive trend in right Salience/Ventral Attention
(𝑟= 0.473, 𝑃= 0.054) and weaker effects elsewhere. Thus, aligned NeuroCogMap parcels showed both
functional consistency and partial preservation of within-network representational structure in higher-order
cortical systems.
Together, these analyses reveal multilevel correspondence between NeuroCogMap and the human cortex.
NeuroCogMap parcel activity predicted cortical responses, and the best-aligned parcels showed both functional
consistency and partial preservation of within-network representational geometry. NeuroCogMap therefore
provides a structured basis for relating LLM internal organization to the functional architecture of the human
cortex.
NeuroCogMap enables cognitively grounded model discovery from LLMs
Model-based fMRI and computational cognitive neuroscience show that neural constraints can help distinguish
latent decision mechanisms that behaviour alone may underdetermine, including model-based inference, model-
free prediction errors and strategy arbitration [59, 60, 61, 55]. Having established measurable correspondence
between NeuroCogMap parcel activity and human cortical responses, we asked whether internal signatures
observed during LLM-based behavioural simulation could support the discovery of explicit cognitive mechanisms.
We first established that these signatures captured decision-relevant variation in human neural activity and in the
ability of LLMs to reproduce individual behaviour (Fig. 6a–c). On this basis, we then translated behaviour-linked
internal signatures into candidate mechanisms for refining a classical cognitive model (Fig. 6d–f).

Figure 6 | NeuroCogMap enables cognitively grounded model discovery from LLMs. a, Prediction of human neural activity during
the two-step decision-making task using fMRI data from [55]. Bars show mean participant-wise prediction accuracy across 10 condition-
stratified participants; error bars denote 95% bootstrap confidence intervals. Benjamini–Hochberg-adjusted 𝑞values indicate Fisher-𝑧-based
comparisons between each baseline and NeuroCogMap. b, Prediction of behavioural fit across five cognitive experiments. Bars show
Pearson correlations between NeuroCogMap-derived representations and LLM behavioural fit across subjects. Significance was assessed by
two-sided tests of Pearson correlation against 𝜌= 0; ∗𝑃< 0.05, ∗∗𝑃< 0.01, ∗∗∗𝑃< 0.001. c, Parcel-level interpretation of behavioural-fit
prediction. For each task family, parcels are ranked by their parcel-wise correlation with AIC-based behavioural fit and grouped into
beneficial and interfering contributors. Parcel descriptions summarize the cognitive processes associated with a stronger or weaker fit.
Beneficial and interfering contributors correspond to negative and positive parcel-wise correlations with AIC, respectively; lower AIC
indicates better behavioural fit. d, Held-out evaluation on two independent two-step task datasets. Bars show AIC values for the classical
cognitive model, the behaviour-discovered model and the NeuroCogMap-discovered model; lower AIC indicates better fit. Two Step Task
One used data from [56] (51,500 trials, 206 participants), and Two Step Task Two used data from [57] (40,000 trials, 100 participants).
Dashed lines indicate random-guessing baselines following [58]. e, NeuroCogMap-guided cognitive-model discovery pipeline. Behavioural
data, baseline model fits and NeuroCogMap latent activations are analysed by a reasoning agent to propose candidate strategies, which are
converted by a model-writing agent into executable cognitive-model code. f, Schematic summary of NeuroCogMap-guided refinement
of the Dual-systems Model, linking behavioural evidence and NeuroCogMap-derived interpretability signals to model extensions and
improved behavioural fit.
NeuroCogMap predicts neural responses during two-step decision-making
We first asked whether NeuroCogMap captured neural variation during the same decision paradigm used for
subsequent model discovery. Using two-step-task fMRI data [55], we predicted participant-wise brain activity
from NeuroCogMap parcel activations with the same encoding framework used in Fig. 5. For computational
efficiency, we analysed a condition-stratified subset of 10 participants, each contributing approximately 300
choices from either the magic-carpet or abstract task version (See Methods). NeuroCogMap achieved the highest
13


## Page 14


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
prediction accuracy among all compared representations (mean 𝑟= 0.355; Fig. 6a), outperforming Word2Vec
(𝑟= 0.205), BERT (𝑟= 0.339), Language (Context) (𝑟= 0.344) and Language (Standard) (𝑟= 0.341). Thus,
NeuroCogMap parcel activations contained information predictive of human neural responses during two-step
decision-making, providing an external neural test of their relevance to the target cognitive domain.
NeuroCogMap predicts behavioural fit across cognitive tasks
We next asked whether NeuroCogMap representations captured variation in how closely the LLM behavioural
simulator reproduced individual human behavioural responses across five diverse cognitive experiments.
These experiments were drawn from the Psych-101 dataset [58] and spanned episodic long-term memory,
multi-attribute decision-making, Shepard categorization, intertemporal choice and drifting four-armed bandit
learning [62, 63, 64, 65, 66]. We quantified behavioural fit using the Akaike information criterion (AIC) [67],
with lower AIC indicating closer correspondence to human behaviour, and tested whether NeuroCogMap parcel
activations predicted this fit across subjects.
Across all five task families, NeuroCogMap-derived representations significantly predicted behavioural fit
(Fig. 6b). Prediction was strongest for episodic long-term memory (𝑟= 0.598), followed by multi-attribute
decision-making (𝑟= 0.556), Shepard categorization (𝑟= 0.522) and intertemporal choice (𝑟= 0.508). A
weaker but significant association was also observed for the drifting four-armed bandit task (𝑟= 0.213). Thus,
NeuroCogMap parcel activity predicted which LLMs better approximated human behaviour, complementing
the neural correspondence observed above.
Parcel-level analyses revealed the internal configurations underlying these behavioural-fit predictions
(Fig. 6c). Parcels associated with better fit supported task-appropriate computations, including uncertainty
tracking in the drifting bandit task and verification/entity retrieval in episodic memory, whereas poorer fit
reflected task-misaligned or overly local processes such as shortcut retrieval, premature answer closure or
irrelevant perceptual-detail extraction. Thus, NeuroCogMap did not reveal a single global signature of human-
like behaviour, but resolved task-specific internal configurations that made an LLM a better or worse behavioural
model.
NeuroCogMap guides refinement of classical cognitive models
Having linked NeuroCogMap to neural activity and behavioural fit, we asked whether the same latent structure
could guide explicit refinement of cognitive models. In the two-step task, a standard paradigm for dissociating
competing decision strategies, we compared a classical Dual-systems Model with LLM-based behavioural
simulators. In the training set, we identified participants for whom the LLM fit human choices better than the
classical model, as indexed by lower AIC, and used NeuroCogMap to identify the parcel- and capability-level
strategies associated with this behavioural advantage (Fig. 6e, see Methods).
The baseline Dual-systems Model assumes arbitration between model-based and model-free valuation,
but does not explicitly capture omission, uncertainty-dependent control or higher-order policy switching. Be-
havioural traces alone suggested richer decision dynamics in LLM-favoured participants, whereas NeuroCogMap
linked this advantage to latent processes including outcome tracking, uncertainty-aware arbitration and policy
switching (Fig. 6f). Guided by these signals, we extended the classical model with interpretable components
targeting omission, dynamic arbitration, history dependence and rule-like switching.
The resulting NeuroCogMap-discovered model yielded better held-out fits than both the original cognitive
model and a comparison model discovered from behavioural traces alone. On Two Step Task One [56],
it achieved the lowest AIC (262.45), outperforming both the original model (268.77) and the behaviour-
only discovered model (268.73). On Two Step Task Two [57], it again achieved the lowest AIC (465.95),
improving over the original model (478.29) and behaviour-only discovered model (473.53; Fig. 6d). The
behaviour-only comparison used the same agents, search budget and screening protocol, and AIC penalized
implementation-level parameter counts. The lower held-out AIC therefore indicates that parcel- and capability-
level NeuroCogMap summaries provided additional information beyond behavioural traces alone.
Together, these findings show that NeuroCogMap can do more than interpret LLM internal states. By
exposing latent computational strategies that are not directly transparent from behaviour alone, it provides a
route for translating LLM internal organization into explicit, testable refinements of cognitive models.
14


## Page 15


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Discussion
NeuroCogMap provides a system-level map of cognitive organization in large language models. By linking
internal features to functional parcels, cognitive descriptions, capability mappings and a cognitive hierarchy, it
defines a mesoscopic scale that is coarse enough to reveal regularities across models, yet specific enough to
support prediction, perturbation and behavioural interpretation. This reframes interpretability as a cognitive-
neuroscience-style analysis of artificial systems, connecting internal computation to behaviour and pathology
and relating artificial organization to human cortical function and cognitive modelling [10, 12, 13].
The pathology analyses show why this organizational layer is explanatory rather than merely descriptive.
Hallucination and social bias were associated with disruptions in representational selection, evaluation and
coordination, whereas refusal failure and sycophancy reflected failures in regulating whether represented
content was expressed, inhibited or independently evaluated [68, 37, 8, 69, 70, 71]. These findings suggest that
model failures are not uniformly reducible to uncertainty, surface errors or generic hidden-state separability,
but can arise from separable breakdowns in the recruitment and coordination of internal cognitive systems. The
same multilevel signatures that distinguished these failures also supported detection and mechanism-guided
steering. NeuroCogMap therefore does not claim to solve alignment or safety; rather, it shows how diagnosis
and intervention can be grounded in internal cognitive organization instead of relying only on output-level
labels or post hoc filtering.
Beyond its role in analysing model pathology, NeuroCogMap provides a way to relate artificial and biological
cognition without assuming mechanistic equivalence between language models and brains. The human-
alignment analyses showed that NeuroCogMap-derived representations predicted cortical responses and
exhibited functional correspondence with higher-order cortical systems associated with semantic integration,
control and context-dependent interpretation. The model-discovery analyses further showed that internal
model organization can reveal candidate latent strategies that, when incorporated into explicit cognitive models,
improve accounts of human decision-making. LLMs are therefore treated not as replicas of human cognition,
but as manipulable artificial systems whose internal structure can be used to generate hypotheses about
cognitive organization and test them against neural and behavioural data [4, 43, 54, 61]. This positions LLMs
as experimental systems for testing principles of organized function across artificial and biological intelligence.
NeuroCogMap bridges brain-inspired artificial intelligence and mechanistic interpretability by using
cognitive neuroscience as a methodological template rather than only as a source of architectural analo-
gies [1, 72, 9, 10, 12, 13]. Task-evoked mapping, functional parcellation, cognitive atlases, representational
analysis, pathology-based inference and causal intervention together enable LLMs to be studied as organized
cognitive systems, rather than treated either as behavioural black boxes or as collections of isolated local
mechanisms [16, 73, 74, 54, 20]. Using cognitive neuroscience as a methodological template nevertheless
requires a clear distinction between biological and artificial parcels. Cortical parcellations divide spatially
continuous tissue into localized regions, whereas NeuroCogMap parcels are non-spatial sets of latent features
grouped primarily by functional response similarity, with layer structure used only as a regularizing constraint,
and may therefore span transformer layers [16, 15]. Their correspondence with the brain is tested at the level of
functional organization, not spatial or mechanistic equivalence, consistent with the distributed and overlapping
organization of human cognition [18, 17]. The same qualification applies to cognitive capabilities: shared task
labels do not imply computational equivalence across systems. Information requiring memory encoding and
later recall in humans, for example, may remain directly accessible in an LLM context window and therefore
require contextual retrieval rather than memory [75]. NeuroCogMap consequently defines capabilities from
LLM-specific tasks as task-grounded, distributed functional constructs rather than localized modules or direct
counterparts of human cognitive faculties.
Several boundaries qualify these conclusions. NeuroCogMap was evaluated on representative Gemma
and LLaMA models spanning pretrained and instruction-tuned stages [24, 25], and the cross-model analyses
indicate that the recovered organization is not specific to a single checkpoint or training regime. Extending the
framework to larger models, additional architectures and broader post-training procedures will be important
for separating stable organizational principles from architecture-, scale- and task-specific contingencies. A
related limitation is that the present analyses focus on language models rather than multimodal systems. This
choice reflects the current maturity of text-only transformer architectures, where shared design principles and
large-scale training regimes have produced relatively stable internal organization. By contrast, multimodal
models still vary substantially in how visual, auditory or other sensory streams are encoded, aligned and
15


## Page 16


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
integrated with language [76, 77, 78]. Applying NeuroCogMap to such systems will therefore be an important
next step, especially for testing whether functional parcels extend across modalities or whether multimodal
cognition requires distinct forms of cross-modal organization. Future work could extend NeuroCogMap from
static maps toward dynamic and causal models of internal state trajectories, directed interactions and systematic
perturbations [79, 80, 81]. Such progress would move the study of LLMs toward a cognitive neuroscience
of artificial intelligence, in which cognitive organization becomes an experimentally tractable object across
artificial and biological systems.
Methods
Construction of NeuroCogMap
NeuroCogMap maps internal cognitive organization in large language models (LLMs) through four linked
representations: functional parcels, parcel-level cognitive descriptions, capability-level mappings and a hierarchy
of cognitive operations. The framework was constructed in four stages. First, we curated a capability space and
associated evaluation datasets from recent LLM literature. Second, we extracted sparse autoencoder (SAE)
activations from LLMs over these datasets and clustered SAE features according to their response profiles to
obtain parcel-like functional units. Third, we assigned each parcel an interpretable cognitive description from
its high-activation examples and linked parcels to a curated capability inventory using activation, intervention
and semantic evidence. Fourth, we organized capabilities into a hierarchy spanning perception, representation,
abstraction and application.
Literature corpus and capability curation
We assembled a corpus of publicly available conference-paper records and metadata for major AI conferences
between 2023 and 2025, including ACL, ICLR, NeurIPS and ICML, using sources such as OpenReview [82]
when available. Records were retained when their metadata or text contained “large language model” or closely
related variants, yielding 5,683 LLM-related papers. From this corpus, we extracted candidate capability terms,
benchmark datasets and task descriptions. Candidate terms were retained when they denoted model abilities,
cognitive operations, evaluation targets or task families, rather than model names or implementation details
alone. Semantically overlapping terms were merged into a normalized capability inventory, and each capability
was linked to benchmark datasets that operationalized the corresponding ability in the source literature. The
final capability space contained 45 cognitive capabilities. Capability definitions and dataset mappings are
reported in Supplementary Tables 8 and 9.
Models and sparse feature extraction
Unless otherwise stated, analyses used Gemma2-2B, Gemma2-9B-IT and Llama-3.1-8B [23, 24, 25]. Gemma2-
2B and Llama-3.1-8B were treated as pretrained base models, and Gemma2-9B-IT as an instruction-tuned model.
Examples were represented as question–answer pairs. For base models, the question and target answer were
concatenated directly; for the instruction-tuned model, the same content was converted to the model-specific
chat template. Inputs were truncated to 1,024 tokens and inference was run in bfloat16 precision.
We represented internal activity in SAE feature space rather than raw neuron space, because individual
model neurons are often polysemantic and can respond to multiple unrelated features, making neuron-level
activations difficult to assign to stable functional roles [10]. SAEs provide a sparse, overcomplete latent basis
that aims to decompose such polysemantic activations into more separable and interpretable feature directions.
For a residual-stream vector hℓ,𝑡∈ℝ𝑑model at layer ℓand token position 𝑡, the SAE for that layer encoded sparse
latent activations and reconstructed the residual stream as

targeted intervention through decoder directions. Consistent with this choice, the function-predictability
validation in Fig. 2b1 included a neuron-based baseline, which performed worse than the SAE-feature-derived
parcel representation on held-out datasets. Gemma2-2B used Gemma Scope residual-stream SAEs for layers
0–25, Gemma2-9B-IT used Gemma Scope residual-stream SAEs for layers 9, 20 and 31 [23], and Llama-3.1-8B
used Llama Scope LXR-8x SAEs for layers 0–31 [83]. Gemma SAEs contained 16,384 latents per layer, and
Llama SAEs contained 32,768 latents per layer.
Activations were extracted over the answer span. For each example, non-special tokens overlapping the
answer were identified from tokenizer offsets, split into sentence-level units and averaged within each sentence.
Sentence-level SAE activation vectors were stored as sparse matrices together with question, answer, sentence-
boundary, token-boundary and dataset metadata. The functional atlas used sentence-level samples as its primary
observations; dataset- and example-level summaries were obtained by token-count-weighted averaging.
Functional atlas construction
For each model, a sample-by-feature activation matrix 𝐴was assembled from sentence-level SAE activations.
Rows corresponded to retained sentence samples and columns corresponded to concatenated SAE features
across selected layers. To ensure that the atlas reflected informative and task-responsive SAE latents rather
than inactive, near-constant or broadly unselective features, we screened features before clustering. Features
were retained only if their non-zero activation rate exceeded a model-specific threshold and their variance
exceeded 10−6. The non-zero activation threshold was 0.03 for Gemma models and 0.01 for Llama-3.1-8B.
Samples whose total activation fell in the bottom 10% were removed. For each feature activation profile 𝑥, we
computed
𝐺(𝑥) = 1 −mean(|𝑥|)2
mean(𝑥2) .
This quantity is a selectivity index for the activation profile: it is low when a feature responds broadly across
samples and high when activation is concentrated in a small subset of samples. Features below the specified
selectivity threshold were removed; the retained quantile was 0.5 for Gemma2-2B and 0.8 for Gemma2-9B-IT
and Llama-3.1-8B. The remaining feature profiles were L2-normalized and reduced by truncated singular value
decomposition, with the number of components chosen as the smallest value explaining at least 80% of the
variance.
SAE features were clustered by minimizing an assignment cost that combined functional similarity and
layer-distribution regularization. For feature 𝑗in layer ℓ𝑗and candidate parcel 𝑝, the assignment cost was
𝐽( 𝑗, 𝑝) = 1 −cos(𝑧𝑗, 𝜇𝑝) + 𝜆layer
∑︁
ℓ
𝑟𝑝,ℓ|ℓ−ℓ𝑗|,
where 𝑧𝑗is the normalized embedding of feature 𝑗, 𝜇𝑝is the normalized mean embedding of parcel 𝑝, and 𝑟𝑝,ℓ
is the current fraction of features in parcel 𝑝assigned to layer ℓ. The second term penalizes assigning a feature
to parcels whose current layer distribution is far from the feature’s layer, encouraging layer-coherent but not
layer-exclusive parcels. We used 𝜆layer = 0.01. Empty parcels were reinitialized with real feature vectors, and
computations were chunked over features to reduce memory use.
For the primary Gemma2-2B atlas, candidate parcel numbers 𝑘= 10, 20, . . . , 300 were evaluated. Clustering
quality was quantified using the gap statistic [84], functional-description quality was estimated from LLM-
assisted expert scoring of parcel descriptions, and non-redundancy was estimated from semantic similarity and
LLM confirmation of redundant parcel descriptions. The integrated score was
𝐶(𝑘) = 0.4 g
Gap(𝑘) + 0.3 e𝑄(𝑘) + 0.3 [1 −e𝑅(𝑘)],
where 𝑄(𝑘) denotes functional-description quality, 𝑅(𝑘) denotes redundancy and tildes denote min–max
normalization across candidate 𝑘values. The maximum occurred at 𝑘= 270. Hyperparameter stability was
evaluated across 16 settings obtained by varying SVD variance retention and clustering iterations; results are
reported in Supplementary Table 4. Latent-to-parcel assignment summaries are reported in Supplementary
Table 12. For this score, 𝑄(𝑘) was computed from structured LLM ratings of the evaluated parcel descriptions,
using the same evidence provided during parcel annotation: activation-weighted keywords, dataset-distribution
summaries and representative high-activation examples. The rubric asked whether each description was specific,
17

NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
faithful to the high-activation examples and covered the dominant activation pattern, with ratings rescaled to a
common 0–1 range before averaging across evaluated parcels. Redundancy 𝑅(𝑘) was estimated by screening
parcel-description pairs with semantic similarity and then using the same structured LLM judgement procedure
to confirm whether pairs described identical or highly overlapping functions. Thus, 𝐶(𝑘) combined one
unsupervised clustering criterion with two interpretation-oriented criteria, and the LLM-assisted components
were used only to choose a stable descriptive granularity rather than as independent validation of the atlas. A
targeted blinded human audit of sampled parcel descriptions is reported in Supplementary Table 6.
Structural connectome
In brain-network analysis, structural connectivity matrices summarize estimated connection strengths among
parcellated regions and provide a substrate for modelling system-level organization [85]. We constructed
an analogous model-level structural connectome by estimating directed relationships among NeuroCogMap
parcels. Because each parcel contains sparse features distributed across model layers, parcel-level connections
were computed by first estimating forward cross-layer relationships between the features assigned to each
parcel pair and then aggregating these relationships at the parcel level.
For a source SAE latent 𝑓in layer ℓ𝑓and a target SAE latent 𝑔in layer ℓ𝑔, only forward cross-layer pairs
satisfying ℓ𝑓< ℓ𝑔were used. The feature-level edge was

the connection strength was set to zero. The resulting matrix was used to constrain interpretation of functional
connectivity during pathology analyses.
Cognitive atlas construction
The cognitive atlas assigns each functional parcel a natural-language description of its dominant computation.
For each parcel, up to 1,000 high-activation samples were retained for keyword extraction and dataset-
distribution estimation, and the top 50 examples were displayed to the annotating model. If a question
exceeded 200 tokens, it was summarized before insertion into the prompt; if summarization failed, it was
truncated to 200 tokens. Keywords were extracted from the concatenated question, answer and activated
sentence using activation-weighted TF–IDF with unigrams and bigrams. The top 20 keywords were retained,
and the top 15 were included in the annotation prompt.
The annotation input contained activation-weighted keywords, dataset-distribution summaries and repre-
sentative high-activation samples. The annotating model returned a concise function name, a 100–200 word
function description and a short account of the parcel’s role in model behaviour. Unless otherwise specified,
LLM-assisted annotation, redundancy review, parcel ranking, cross-model parcel judgement, pathology la-
belling and human–LLM functional correspondence judgement used GPT-OSS-20B [86] served through a local
inference endpoint, with structured outputs required where downstream parsing was needed. GPT-5.2 [87] was
used only for the model-discovery agents described below. The resulting Gemma2-2B parcel atlas is reported in
Supplementary Table 10. Prompt templates, input fields and output schemas are provided in the Supplementary
prompt templates section.
We performed a targeted blinded human audit of judgement-dependent components rather than full manual
relabelling. Three annotators independently evaluated sampled annotation files, including 60 Gemma2-2B
parcel descriptions, 570 pathology-labelled responses and 100 cross-model matched parcel pairs. Parcel
descriptions were rated for specificity, faithfulness to high-activation examples, coverage of the dominant
18


## Page 19


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
activation pattern and overreach; pathology labels and cross-model matches were evaluated with task-specific
rubrics. Categorical disagreements were summarized by majority vote, ordinal ratings by the mean across
annotators, and no-majority or ambiguous cases were retained as such or excluded from coarse agreement
calculations where specified. Inter-annotator agreement and audit outcomes are reported in Supplementary
Table 6. Thus, the LLM-assisted components supported semantic annotation and organization, whereas key
validation endpoints were empirical activation rankings, cross-model activation correspondence, perturbation
effects and blinded human audit.
Parcel–capability mapping and hierarchy
Adapting the logic of functional parcellation in cognitive neuroscience to model activations, NeuroCogMap
defines parcels as non-spatial, mesoscopic functional units in SAE-feature space: groups of sparse features with
similar task-evoked response profiles and coherent functional annotations. Capabilities summarize higher-
order task-evoked functional dimensions supported by weighted parcel sets, and the hierarchy provides a
coarse operational ordering from perceptual access and representation to abstract reasoning and situated
application. Parcel–capability links combined task-evoked activation, intervention evidence and semantic
consistency. Candidate capability–dataset links were inherited from the same source papers used to define the
capability inventory: when a paper described a capability together with benchmark tasks or evaluation datasets,
those datasets were treated as operationalizations of that capability. These automatically extracted links were
manually reviewed to remove model names, implementation details and weak or ambiguous dataset–capability
associations. For dataset 𝑑, parcel 𝑝and sample set 𝑋𝑑, dataset-level parcel activation was

Detector features therefore aggregated evidence from parcels positively assigned to a capability, whereas
comparatively suppressive associations were retained only in the signed atlas-level map.
Capabilities were organized into four hierarchy levels: perception, representation, abstraction and appli-
cation. Assignments were informed by semantic relations among capability definitions and Bloom-inspired
distinctions in cognitive complexity, then reviewed for consistency with the operational definitions of the four
levels. Capability-level definitions and assignments are reported in Supplementary Table 7.
Validation of atlas organization
We evaluated NeuroCogMap organization at the level of parcels, parcel descriptions, parcel–capability mappings
and capability hierarchy. Semantic homogeneity of the functional atlas (Fig. 2a2) was tested using the ten
highest-activating examples for each parcel. When an activated sentence was marked, only that sentence was
embedded; otherwise, the full sample content was used. Texts were embedded with Qwen3-Embedding-8B
and L2-normalized. Within-parcel similarity was the mean pairwise cosine similarity among selected texts,
and between-parcel similarity was the mean similarity between texts in the parcel and selected texts from all
other parcels. Within- and between-parcel similarities were compared across parcels using a paired Wilcoxon
signed-rank test.
Cross-LLM correspondence and functional similarity of matched parcels (Fig. 2a3,b3) used parcel function
descriptions to align source and target parcels. Candidate target parcels were ranked by Qwen3-Embedding-
8B semantic similarity; the five highest-scoring candidates were then evaluated by GPT-OSS-20B. The final
alignment score was
𝐻(𝑝, 𝑞) = 0.4 𝐸(𝑝, 𝑞) + 0.6 𝐽(𝑝, 𝑞),
where 𝐸(𝑝, 𝑞) is embedding cosine similarity rescaled to [0, 1], and 𝐽(𝑝, 𝑞) is the LLM-judged functional
similarity score on the same scale. For each matched pair and dataset with sufficient train and test samples, a
scalar linear regression model was fit on the training split and evaluated by Pearson correlation on the held-out
split. Sentence-level examples were sampled from datasets shared by the source and target models with fixed
random seeds for train and test selection. Random non-matched target parcels were used as a baseline.
Predictability of parcel activation from functional descriptions (Fig. 2b1) tested whether functional de-
scriptions recovered empirical parcel activation rankings on held-out data. This analysis used the primary
Gemma2-2B atlas containing 270 parcels and evaluated 40 held-out datasets with up to 500 examples per
20


## Page 21


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
dataset. Each example and parcel text was embedded with Qwen3-Embedding-8B [53]; the ten highest-
similarity parcels were then passed to GPT-OSS-20B [86], served through a local vLLM endpoint [88], which
assigned activation probabilities using temperature 0.3 and structured output. Ground-truth rankings were
computed from held-out SAE activations after setting negative activations to zero. Prediction accuracy was quan-
tified by Pearson correlation against these held-out empirical rankings. Baselines included random-clustering,
neuron-based and keyword-only controls when available, and paired two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank tests
with Holm correction were performed across datasets.
Targeted parcel intervention (Fig. 2b2) tested whether positive steering of an individual parcel shifted
generated outputs towards that parcel’s assigned function. For each parcel, decoder directions for all assigned
SAE latents were added at the corresponding residual-stream hook points during prompt processing and autore-
gressive decoding. Fig. 2b2 summarizes the factual question-answering examples sampled from HaluEval [29]
for evaluation, with up to 100 examples assigned to each parcel. Inputs were truncated to 1,024 tokens,
generations were capped at 128 new tokens and decoding was greedy. The primary scoring mode embedded
the parcel description and the pre- and post-intervention responses with Qwen3-Embedding-8B and compared
cosine similarity after rescaling to [0, 1]. GPT-OSS-20B scoring was retained as an optional judge-based check
rather than the primary score. For multi-strength analyses, the post-intervention score for an example was the
maximum score observed across the prespecified positive-strength grid; examples whose generated text did not
change were excluded from the intervention-effect calculation. This maximum-over-strength summary was
used only to evaluate parcel-function consistency, not to select the reported pathology-mitigation strength.
Activation selectivity of parcel–capability links and performance change after perturbation (Fig. 2c1,c2)
were tested by asking whether high-scoring links were selectively activated by the corresponding capability
and whether perturbing linked parcels caused larger changes in answer log probability than perturbing
unrelated controls. Correlation-based analyses used held-out test examples and SAE activations where available,
comparing capability-relevant datasets with matched low-relevance datasets or comparing high-, low- and
random-ranked parcel groups on capability-relevant datasets. Perturbation analyses used the same selected
parcel groups and measured changes in the conditional log probability of answer tokens only, with inputs
truncated to 1,024 tokens and up to 128 examples per dataset. Suppressive ablation used steering strength
−1.0, and positive sufficiency steering used strength 0.5 in the main Fig. 2c summaries. Random controls were
sampled after excluding the high-related parcels using a fixed seed.
Semantic organization across hierarchy layers (Fig. 2d1) was tested by asking whether parcels mapped to
a hierarchy level were semantically closer to their own level description than to other level descriptions. For
each capability, the top ten ranked parcels were selected from the final Gemma2-2B capability–parcel map;
parcels were pooled within each hierarchy level by parcel identifier, sorted by recurrence across capabilities
and truncated to at most 20 unique parcels per level. Parcel texts and four fixed hierarchy descriptions
were embedded with Qwen3-Embedding-8B and compared by cosine similarity. Extended hierarchy analyses
quantified semantic dispersion as one minus the mean pairwise cosine similarity among top-ranked parcel
descriptions pooled by hierarchy level, and structural degree after thresholding the model-level parcel–parcel
connectome at the strongest 10% of off-diagonal absolute weights and summarizing it as an undirected,
unweighted graph.
Capability prerequisite learning across hierarchy layers (Fig. 2d2) was evaluated with controlled synthetic
training experiments separating one-hop in-context retrieval, one-hop parametric recall and two-hop reasoning.
This analysis tested whether estimated capability dependencies could predict staged learning dynamics in
a setting where prerequisite relations were controlled. The task design was inspired by controlled synthetic
knowledge-storage experiments in the Physics of Language Models framework [89]. Synthetic person profiles
contained a name and five attributes: birth date, birth city, university, major and company. These profiles
generated biography text, one-hop QA examples and two-hop relational questions; representative examples
are provided in the Supplementary Information. GPT-NeoX/Pythia models at 70M, 160M and 410M scale
were initialized from configuration rather than pretrained weights, so the experiment measured controlled
synthetic training rather than continued pretraining. Models used AdamW [90], cosine learning-rate schedules,
mixed precision, gradient clipping and gradient checkpointing. The 70M and 160M models were trained for
40,000 steps with 1,000 warmup steps, batch size 96, maximum learning rate 10−3 and minimum learning
rate 10−4; the 410M model used 40,000 steps, 5,000 warmup steps, batch size 32 with gradient accumulation
4, maximum learning rate 5 × 10−5 and minimum learning rate 10−5. Held-out generation used temperature 0,
top-𝑝= 1 and maximum length 50, and a response was counted as correct if it contained the gold answer string.
21

NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Plotted summaries used five repeated runs for 70M, 160M and 410M; pairwise comparisons used Welch’s
two-sample 𝑡-tests.
Pathology analysis, detection and intervention
Hallucination, social bias, refusal failure and sycophancy were analysed with a common contrastive framework.
Labels followed the original dataset annotations or evaluation protocols and were collapsed into normative
and pathological conditions for the contrastive analyses: truthful versus hallucinated responses for factual
answering, fair or unbiased versus biased responses for BBQ social-bias examples [91], successful refusal
versus refusal failure for harmful-instruction prompts, and independent versus sycophantic responses for
Sycophancy-Eval [92]. Hallucination labels were assigned by reference-based semantic correctness; BBQ
responses were mapped to uncertainty, stereotype-aligned and non-stereotyped alternatives; refusal labels
separated safe refusal from harmful compliance; and sycophancy labels separated independent answers from
user-influenced shifts in Answer and Feedback settings. Dataset-specific roles, sample sizes and representative
task patterns are provided in Supplementary Table 1 and the Supplementary Information.
For each prompt-response pair, SAE activations were extracted over the generated answer span and mapped
to parcels. Let X𝑖∈ℝ𝑇𝑖×𝑃denote the parcel trajectory for sample 𝑖, where 𝑇𝑖is the number of answer tokens
and 𝑃is the number of parcels. The sample-level parcel activation vector was

For detection, features were constructed from the generated answer span, so the detector was evaluated as
a response-level classifier of completed generations rather than as a pre-generation monitor. Features were
derived from the parcel activation vector, parcel functional-connectivity matrix, capability activation vector and
capability functional-connectivity matrix. Pathology-relevant signatures were selected within each training fold
by a prespecified top-𝑘rule applied to training-fold contrastive statistics: up to ten normative-shifted parcels,
ten pathology-shifted parcels, ten normative-shifted capabilities, ten pathology-shifted capabilities and the top
25 normative- and pathology-shifted differential edges at the parcel and capability connectivity levels. The
selected signature indices were then applied unchanged to the held-out fold. Hierarchy-level profiles were
used for interpretation and visualization but not as an additional classifier feature block.
Each response was represented by a 12-dimensional interpretable feature vector. Let 𝑃+, 𝑃−, 𝐶+ and 𝐶−

The final four prototype-alignment features compared the response with normative and pathological training-
fold prototypes:

NeuroCogMap detectors used the same logistic-regression classifier, implemented with L2 regularization and
class-balanced weights.
Evaluation was performed separately for each pathology dataset and model using stratified fivefold cross-
validation with shuffle enabled and random seed 42. Signature selection, normative and pathological prototypes,
feature standardization and logistic-regression fitting were estimated within the training fold only. AUROC
was the primary detection metric and was computed from held-out predicted probabilities. Pathology-specific
baselines used the same labels and cross-validation splits and are described in the Supplementary Information.
Mechanism-guided mitigation converted significant signed parcel abnormalities into generation-time steering
targets. Under the normative-minus-pathological convention, positive parcels were treated as enhancement
targets because they were under-recruited in pathological responses, whereas negative parcels were treated
as suppression targets because they were over-recruited in pathological responses. We therefore selected the
three most positive significant parcels for enhancement and the three most negative significant parcels for
suppression. If fewer significant parcels were available in either direction, all available significant parcels in
that direction were used. Let T denote the selected steering-target set and let Δ𝑝≡Δa,𝑝denote the parcel-level
activation contrast for parcel 𝑝. For each 𝑝∈T, the signed steering coefficient was
