The intervention-strength grid was 𝜂∈{0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 1.0}. For each dataset, the steering strength was selected
on an independent validation split using the task-specific evaluation metric and then fixed before evaluation on
the held-out test split. Baseline and steered generations used the same prompts and were evaluated with the
same task-specific evaluator. Unless otherwise specified, generation used greedy decoding with temperature 0
and a maximum of 128 new tokens.
23


## Page 24


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Human cortical prediction and correspondence
Human–LLM correspondence was evaluated with the LeBel story-listening fMRI dataset, in which eight
participants listened to extended autobiographical narratives. Analyses used released preprocessed cortical
responses, projected to the Schaefer100 parcellation and summarized by Yeo seven-network assignments.
Participants were included only if their mean framewise displacement was below 0.2 mm. NeuroCogMap
features were extracted from Gemma2-2B with the 270-parcel atlas. Model inputs used a 256-token preceding
context. NeuroCogMap features were downsampled to fMRI repetition times and expanded with finite impulse
response delays. Feature and response matrices were standardized within story and participant.
Ridge-regression encoding models predicted held-out cortical responses from all NeuroCogMap parcels
jointly. Following the published evaluation protocol [47], the final story, Where There’s Smoke, was held out
for evaluation. Ridge regularization was selected by inner cross-validation on training stories over 10−1 to
108, separately for each cortical target. Prediction accuracy was Pearson correlation between predicted and
observed held-out responses, Fisher-𝑧-transformed for averaging and back-transformed for reporting. Baselines
included WordRate, Word2Vec, BERT base uncased, Gemma2-2B Language Context and Gemma2-2B Language
Standard. All baselines were implemented within the same encoding framework [50], using identical story
splits, temporal downsampling, finite impulse response delays, standardization, ridge fitting and held-out
evaluation; only the input feature representation was replaced. The NeuroCogMap representation used only 270
parcel-level predictors, providing a compact, low-dimensional encoding space relative to hidden-state language
baselines, which used substantially higher-dimensional model representations such as 2,304-dimensional
Gemma2-2B hidden-state features before temporal expansion. Thus, its prediction performance reflects the
ability of functionally organized parcels to capture brain-relevant variance with a comparatively small set of
interpretable predictors.
Functional correspondence was tested by comparing the cognitive profile of each cortical parcel with
the NeuroCogMap parcels that best predicted it. Each Schaefer100 parcel was assigned a cognitive profile
from Neurosynth meta-analytic associations with 123 Cognitive Atlas terms. The 50 terms with the largest
absolute 𝑧-scores were converted into a short functional description containing a function name, a 100–200
word description and a role summary. For each human parcel, top-ten and bottom-ten NeuroCogMap parcels
were selected according to held-out prediction accuracy. Similarities between human and NeuroCogMap
descriptions were computed with Qwen3-Embedding-8B, averaged within each human parcel and summarized
within Yeo networks. A complementary GPT-OSS-20B judge assigned functional-correspondence scores in
[0, 1] using six cognitive-domain criteria. The case-study panel used the held-out story Where There’s Smoke;
candidate correspondences were selected from high prediction-ranked matches and manually curated only to
cover dorsal-attention, default-mode, control-monitoring and social-affective systems, not as an independent
statistical test. For representational similarity analysis, human similarity matrices were constructed from human
functional descriptions and NeuroCogMap matrices from the mean embedding of the top three prediction-
ranked NeuroCogMap descriptions for each human parcel. Matrices were constructed within Salience/Ventral
Attention, Frontoparietal Control and Default networks, separately by hemisphere, and upper-triangular entries
were compared with Pearson correlation. For RSA, Benjamini–Hochberg correction was applied across the
six network–hemisphere comparisons. Prompt templates for cortical-profile description and human–LLM
functional-correspondence judgement are provided in the Supplementary prompt templates section.
Cognitively grounded model discovery
Model-discovery analyses tested whether NeuroCogMap features could guide refinement of classical cognitive
models. We first evaluated whether Gemma2-2B NeuroCogMap parcel activations predicted trial-wise fMRI
responses in a public two-step decision-making dataset [55]. The released Schaefer100 ROI table contained
94 participants, comprising 48 participants in the abstract task version and 46 in the story task version. For
the neural-alignment analysis, ten participants were selected by task-version stratification and coverage of the
model-based to model-free behavioural spectrum, with six from the abstract version and four from the story
version. Included participants had at least two fMRI runs and at least 140 valid trials. The selected subset
spanned a model-based behavioural index from −1.468 to 1.577 and fitted model-based weights from 0.000 to
0.905.
For the neural-prediction analysis, each decision trial was represented by the text prompt and recent trial
24


## Page 25


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
history used for Gemma2-2B activation extraction. Trial-wise beta estimates from Schaefer100 ROIs were
predicted from the resulting 270-dimensional NeuroCogMap parcel-activation features. Within each participant
and cross-validation fold, all ROI targets were regressed jointly in a multivariate ridge fit. The ridge penalty
was selected by nested leave-one-run-out cross-validation over scanning blocks. Inputs were standardized and
reduced by principal component analysis retaining 95% of training-set variance before ridge fitting. Prediction
accuracy was computed separately for each ROI and averaged across ROIs to obtain one participant-level
score. Participant-level scores were Fisher-𝑧-transformed for aggregation, and 95% confidence intervals were
computed by bootstrap resampling participants.
We evaluated five representative cognitive experiments from Psych-101: episodic long-term memory, multi-
attribute decision-making, Shepard categorization, intertemporal choice and a drifting four-armed bandit; task
structures and behavioural sample sizes are summarized in Supplementary Table 5. Gemma2-2B was treated
as a behavioural simulator by assigning likelihoods to candidate human responses from trial-wise prompts.
Participant-level negative log-likelihood was converted to AIC as
AIC = 2𝑘+ 2NLL,
where 𝑘is the number of fitted parameters and NLL is the summed negative log-likelihood over trials. Lower
AIC indicates better fit. Parcel-wise Pearson correlations between participant-level NeuroCogMap activation
and AIC were used to identify beneficial and interfering contributors, with negative correlations indicating
better fit. For the predictive analysis in Fig. 6b, participant-level 270-dimensional NeuroCogMap activation
vectors were used to predict participant-level AIC using the predefined train–test partitions provided with
the Psych-101 datasets. A multivariate linear model was fit on the training participants and evaluated on the
held-out test participants; the reported metric was the held-out Pearson correlation between predicted and
observed AIC, tested two-sided against 𝜌= 0.
Model discovery focused on the two-step task because it is a classic and mechanistically rich sequential
decision-making paradigm with an established Dual-systems Model benchmark. Following the Centaur open-
loop protocol, we used the Psych-101 test-participant split to define held-out participants for each two-
step dataset; these participants were excluded from model discovery, and the remaining participants in the
corresponding original two-step dataset formed the discovery set. NeuroCogMap summaries, LLM-favoured
participant selection and candidate model generation used only discovery-set participants. This analysis builds
on recent model-guided scientific discovery work around Psych-101, including the Centaur multi-attribute
decision-making study, in which foundation-model behavioural predictions were used to guide the refinement of
interpretable cognitive models [58]. We extended this paradigm by supplementing behavioural traces, residuals
and AIC comparisons with NeuroCogMap-derived parcel- and capability-level summaries of the model’s internal
organization. Behavioural regimes were treated as LLM-favoured when the LLM simulator had a lower AIC
than the classical model, with no additional margin. A reasoning agent using GPT-5.2 [87] with temperature
0.7 proposed candidate cognitive mechanisms, and a model-writing agent converted these mechanisms into
executable model extensions. The NeuroCogMap-guided and behaviour-only branches used the same reasoning
agent, model-writing agent, search budget and screening protocol; the behaviour-only branch received no
NeuroCogMap features. Human checking was limited to faithfulness to the proposed mechanisms, executable
syntax, fit compatibility and removal of unsupported claims, and did not substantively rewrite candidate model
proposals.
The baseline model for the two-step model-discovery analysis was the standard Dual-systems Model [61]
for the two-step task. This classical baseline was used as the reference model against which the behaviour-only
and NeuroCogMap-guided extensions were compared. In the implementation used for AIC computation,
the original Dual-systems Model contained 5 trainable parameters, the behaviour-only discovered extension
contained 18 trainable parameters, and the NeuroCogMap-guided extension contained 24 trainable parameters.
The behaviour-only extension included lapse and omission terms, prediction-error-dependent arbitration,
state-specific second-stage win-stay/lose-shift and perseveration, value forgetting, post-omission reset and
adaptive learning-rate terms. The AIC penalty used these implementation-level parameter counts for each
participant-level fit. At the first stage, choices were governed by a mixture of model-based and model-free
values,

from Two Step Task One and Two Step Task Two [56, 57]. Two Step Task One contained 51,500 trials
from 206 participants and Two Step Task Two contained 40,000 trials from 100 participants. Following the
Centaur open-loop fitting protocol, the original Dual-systems Model, behaviour-only discovered model and
NeuroCogMap-discovered model were refit separately for each held-out participant using that participant’s
complete choice sequence; participant-level AIC was computed from the summed negative log-likelihood over
the same sequence. Aggregate AIC was computed as the mean participant-level AIC. Fig. 6d is reported as
held-out participant-level model-comparison evidence rather than as a separate inferential test.

NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Statistical analysis and implementation details
Detailed test statistics, effect sizes and panel-specific sample sizes are reported in the Results, figure legends and
Supplementary Tables. In the Methods, we specify the unit of analysis and inferential family for each analysis
to support reproducibility. Unless otherwise stated, generated responses were the unit for pathology analyses,
parcels or capabilities were the unit for atlas-validation comparisons, cortical parcels were the unit for human
encoding comparisons, and participants were the unit for behavioural model comparisons. Paired tests were
used for matched designs, Welch tests for independent groups with unequal variances and Wilcoxon signed-rank
tests for paired non-parametric comparisons. Multiple comparisons were controlled with Holm-Bonferroni or
Benjamini–Hochberg correction [94, 95] as specified for the corresponding analysis. Error bars denote the
uncertainty measure specified in each figure legend: 95% confidence intervals were computed over the plotted
analysis units unless explicitly described as bootstrap intervals, standard deviations summarize repeated runs
or cross-validation folds where stated, and Fig. 6a uses participant-bootstrap confidence intervals.
All experiments were implemented in Python. Model inference, activation extraction and parcel interventions
used PyTorch-based code with SAE Lens and TransformerLens-compatible hooks. Numerical analysis used
NumPy, SciPy, pandas, scikit-learn and sentence-transformers. Random seeds were fixed at 42 for stochastic
dimensionality reduction, random controls, cross-validation splits and sampling-based validation procedures
unless otherwise stated. Experiments were run on a Linux server with eight NVIDIA RTX A6000 GPUs, dual
Intel Xeon Gold 6346 CPUs, NVIDIA driver 535.274.02 and CUDA 12.2. Exact software and dependency
versions, together with environment-reconstruction instructions, are provided in the public code repository and
its archived release.

Aggregate category analysis of pathology-related activation differences
We further examined whether pathological behaviours differed in the category of NeuroCogMap units they
perturbed. Parcels and capabilities were grouped as belief-related (representation-related) or behavioural-
control-related, and we compared the absolute activation difference between pathological and non-pathological
examples for each group (Extended Data Fig. 7). Hallucination and bias showed larger activation differences
in belief-related parcels and capabilities, consistent with their interpretation as representational pathologies
involving disrupted semantic grounding, knowledge integration or internal consistency. Refusal failure and
sycophancy showed the opposite pattern at the parcel level, with larger activation differences in behavioural-
control-related units. The corresponding capability-level effects were directionally consistent but weaker
and did not reach significance for either behavioural-control pathology. Thus, the category-level analysis
supports a separation between representational and behavioural-control failure modes, while indicating that
this separation is most clearly resolved at the finer parcel granularity.
Social bias reflects misrouting of socially salient representations
We next examined social bias in BBQ [69, 91], focusing first on the disability subset by comparing fair and
biased responses. NeuroCogMap revealed a representational pathology distinct from hallucination. Rather
than reflecting failed truth evaluation or disrupted factual grounding, biased responses were characterized
by abnormal coupling between demographic representations and knowledge-retrieval pathways, whereas fair
responses preferentially recruited structured comparison, neutral inference and higher-order normative control
(Extended Data Fig. 8a–d).
At the circuit level, biased responses were dominated by a demographic-retrieval pathway centred on Demo-
graphic Comparison. The strongest bias-enhanced edges linked Celebrity Knowledge Retrieval to Demographic
Comparison (Δ𝑤= −8.68, 𝑃= 1.8 × 10−24), Wikipedia Fact Retrieval to Demographic Comparison (Δ𝑤= −8.68,
𝑃= 9.3 × 10−35), and Fact Extraction Module to Demographic Comparison (Δ𝑤= −6.91, 𝑃= 2.6 × 10−17). Biased
responses also showed stronger coupling involving the Neutral Inference Module, including Factual Retrieval
Module–Neutral Inference Module (Δ𝑤= −7.28, 𝑃= 3.7 × 10−18) and Neutral Inference Module–Demographic
Comparison (Δ𝑤= −7.34, 𝑃= 1.1 × 10−16). This pattern suggests that bias was not best explained by a lack
of relevant knowledge, but by the way socially salient category information was coupled to factual and ency-
clopaedic retrieval. Fair responses, by contrast, strengthened a structured comparison-and-uncertainty circuit,
including Factoid Retrieval Module–Item Enumeration (Δ𝑤= 7.80, 𝑃< 10−300), Spatial Direction Processing–
Locative Retrieval Module (Δ𝑤= 7.29, 𝑃= 1.1 × 10−32), and Number-Retrieval Module–Locative Retrieval Module

Figure 7 | Category-level activation differences across representational and behavioural-control pathologies. a,b, Absolute parcel-
and capability-level activation differences for two representational pathologies: hallucination (a) and bias (b). For hallucination, activation
contrasts were computed between truthful and hallucinated outputs; for bias, contrasts were computed between fair or unbiased and biased
outputs. c,d, The same analysis for two behavioural-control pathologies: refusal failure (c) and sycophancy (d). For refusal failure, contrasts
were computed between successful refusal and refusal-failed outputs; for sycophancy, contrasts were computed between independent and
sycophantic outputs. Capabilities were defined from a fixed taxonomy of interpretable functional competencies, and parcels were defined
as functionally annotated sparse-autoencoder regions of residual-stream activations. Each capability definition and parcel functional
summary was classified under a fixed prompt-based rubric as belief-related (representation-related), behavioural-control-related, mixed,
neutral or unknown (see the Supplementary prompt templates section). Belief-related units corresponded to functions associated with
semantic grounding, knowledge integration and internal consistency, whereas behavioural-control-related units corresponded to inhibition,
arbitration among competing objectives and output regulation. Only units assigned to the belief-related or behavioural-control-related
categories were retained for the two-group comparison shown here; mixed, neutral and unknown units were excluded. For each retained
parcel or capability, the plotted value denotes the absolute activation contrast between the two conditions, |Δactivation|, so that larger
values indicate stronger differential engagement irrespective of direction. Unit-level contrasts were aggregated across included benchmarks
by taking the maximum absolute contrast across datasets. Violin plots show the distribution across units, overlaid points denote individual
parcels or capabilities, and dashed horizontal lines denote zero activation difference. 𝑃values were computed using two-sample Welch
𝑡-tests comparing belief-related and behavioural-control-related groups; significance stars denote conventional thresholds.
(Δ𝑤= 7.28, 𝑃< 10−300). Fair responses also showed stronger control from Number-Retrieval Module and
Locative Retrieval Module to Uncertainty Reasoner (Δ𝑤= 7.10, 𝑃< 10−300; Δ𝑤= 6.97, 𝑃= 1.5 × 10−43). Thus,
NeuroCogMap separates two circuit regimes: a bias-prone route in which demographic representations are
directly linked to retrieval pathways, and a fair-response route in which the model relies more on comparison,
enumeration and uncertainty-sensitive reasoning (Extended Data Fig. 8a).
Parcel-level activity reinforced this interpretation. Fair responses strongly recruited parcels supporting
explicit comparison and structured evaluation, including Duality Coordination (Δ𝑎= 187.24, 𝑃= 2.6 × 10−298),
Universal Claim Processor (Δ𝑎= 111.98, 𝑃= 4.0 × 10−297), Number-Retrieval Module (Δ𝑎= 78.74, 𝑃=
1.9×10−211), Universal Attribute Reasoning (Δ𝑎= 49.63, 𝑃= 5.1×10−138), Neutral Inference Module (Δ𝑎= 44.97,
𝑃= 5.3 × 10−47) and Social Norm Reasoning (Δ𝑎= 34.42, 𝑃= 6.8 × 10−7). Biased responses instead over-
recruited socially loaded or category-linked factual parcels, including Medical Disease Knowledge (Δ𝑎= −55.61,
𝑃= 3.9 × 10−7), Demographic Comparison (Δ𝑎= −47.49, 𝑃= 4.6 × 10−5), Professional Knowledge Retrieval
(Δ𝑎= −38.29, 𝑃= 7.7 × 10−6), Key-Term Retrieval (Δ𝑎= −30.59, 𝑃= 3.0 × 10−5), Factual Retrieval Module
(Δ𝑎= −23.00, 𝑃= 5.5 × 10−15) and Hate-Speech and Profanity Detector (Δ𝑎= −18.40, 𝑃= 0.0014). In the
disability domain, the co-activation of Medical Disease Knowledge and Demographic Comparison suggests a
specific failure mode: clinically or socially associated category information was treated as relevant evidence for
answering, rather than being recognized as a potentially stereotype-laden cue requiring control (Extended
Data Fig. 8b).

capacities, including Causal reasoning (Δ𝑐= 19.92, 𝑃= 1.1 × 10−68), Comprehension and interpretation

Multi-level NeuroCogMap signatures separating unbiased from biased responses, including anomalous parcel–parcel connectivity (a),
parcel activation shifts (b), capability activation shifts (c) and hierarchy-level activation differences (d). All contrasts use an unbiased-
minus-biased sign; for connectivity, positive values indicate stronger unbiased-response connectivity and negative values indicate stronger
biased-response connectivity. e, Bias detection from NeuroCogMap-derived pathology signatures, measured by AUROC across BBQ subsets
in Gemma2-2B and Gemma2-9B-IT. Insets show average AUROC across subsets. f, Bias mitigation by NeuroCogMap-guided parcel
intervention, measured by fairness accuracy, defined as the unbiased-response rate. Unless otherwise stated, analyses were performed on
Gemma2-2B. Error bars indicate 95% confidence intervals in b,c and standard deviation in e. Statistical significance in b,c was assessed
using two-sided paired 𝑡-tests: *𝑃< 0.05, **𝑃< 0.01, ***𝑃< 0.001. Dataset details and sample sizes are reported in Methods and
Supplementary Table 1; intervention procedures are described in Methods.
(Δ𝑐= 13.63, 𝑃= 2.2 × 10−54), Induction and inference (Δ𝑐= 11.28, 𝑃= 1.3 × 10−45), Analytical thinking
(Δ𝑐= 10.27, 𝑃= 7.2 × 10−42), Ethical reasoning (Δ𝑐= 8.22, 𝑃= 0.0015), Alignment (Δ𝑐= 8.18, 𝑃= 9.5 × 10−6),
Bias mitigation (Δ𝑐= 7.33, 𝑃= 1.1 × 10−5), Judgment (Δ𝑐= 6.15, 𝑃= 6.0 × 10−7) and Verification (Δ𝑐= 4.29,
𝑃= 4.4 × 10−4). Biased responses were relatively stronger in Common sense reasoning (Δ𝑐= −9.72, 𝑃=
2.7 × 10−5), Knowledge recall (Δ𝑐= −5.63, 𝑃= 3.0 × 10−15), Attention (Δ𝑐= −5.50, 𝑃= 2.5 × 10−8), Information
retrieval (Δ𝑐= −4.49, 𝑃= 4.3 × 10−12), Knowledge integration (Δ𝑐= −4.27, 𝑃= 5.6 × 10−10) and Knowledge
extraction (Δ𝑐= −4.25, 𝑃= 6.1 × 10−10). This capability-level dissociation shows that biased responses were
not simply less capable responses. Rather, they reflected a shift from controlled evaluation and normative
judgment towards a more automatic regime dominated by common-sense association, attention and knowledge
retrieval (Extended Data Fig. 8c).
At the hierarchy level, biased responses showed relatively greater activation in Perceptual Access and
Attentional Gating, whereas fair responses shifted towards Abstract Reasoning and Meta-Cognitive Control and
Situated Application and Social Interaction. This pattern suggests that social bias reflects an imbalance between
early access to socially salient category information and higher-order processes that evaluate, compare and
regulate its relevance for the response. Thus, whereas hallucination primarily involved failures in how factual
knowledge was retrieved, monitored or coordinated, bias involved a distinct representational failure in how
social information was routed into normative and context-sensitive decision processes (Extended Data Fig. 8d).
Together, these findings show that social bias in LLMs is not merely a superficial output preference. Neu-
roCogMap reveals a systems-level representational pathology in which socially salient category information be-
comes over-coupled to factual retrieval and common-sense association, while comparison, uncertainty-sensitive
reasoning and normative evaluation are insufficiently recruited. This distinguishes bias from hallucination:
both involve representational failure, but bias arises from how social representations are routed and regulated,
rather than from a general failure of truth evaluation or factual grounding.
39


## Page 40


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
Mechanism-guided detection and mitigation of social bias
Finally, we asked whether the bias-related pathology signatures identified by NeuroCogMap could also support
diagnosis and intervention. Using the same mechanism-guided framework as above, we constructed a detector
from circuit-, parcel- and capability-level bias signatures and compared it with attention probing, hidden probing
and perplexity baselines across four BBQ subdomains: Age, Disability, Gender and Nationality (Extended Data
Fig. 8e). NeuroCogMap achieved near-ceiling discrimination in both models. On Gemma2-2B, it reached a mean
AUROC of 1.000, slightly exceeding hidden probing (0.999) and substantially outperforming perplexity (0.883)
and attention probing (0.534). On Gemma2-9B-IT, NeuroCogMap again achieved near-ceiling performance
(mean AUROC, 0.992), remaining above hidden probing (0.991) and outperforming perplexity (0.733) and
attention probing (0.569). These results indicate that biased outputs are better captured by structured
parcel-level pathology than by generic salience or surprisal measures.
We then derived dataset-specific steering policies from the bias pathology map and tested whether these
signatures contributed to biased response formation (Extended Data Fig. 8f). On Gemma2-2B, NeuroCogMap-
guided intervention produced substantial gains in fairness accuracy, increasing performance from 58.3% to
90.1% on BBQ-Age, from 60.4% to 65.6% on BBQ-Nationality, from 58.2% to 93.7% on BBQ-Gender, and
from 56.2% to 88.8% on BBQ-Disability. Averaged across subdomains, fairness accuracy increased from 58.3%
to 84.5%. On Gemma2-9B-IT, baseline fairness was already higher and intervention effects were smaller
and mixed: fairness accuracy increased from 80.8% to 81.6% on BBQ-Age and from 89.6% to 91.6% on
BBQ-Nationality, but decreased slightly from 88.9% to 88.5% on BBQ-Gender and from 88.4% to 84.8% on
BBQ-Disability. These results suggest that NeuroCogMap-based steering is most effective when substantial
residual bias pathology remains, whereas effects are limited and may be neutral or negative when baseline
fairness is already high.
Together, the hallucination and bias analyses indicate that representational pathologies in LLMs are struc-
tured but not unitary disturbances of internal cognitive organization. Hallucination disrupted factual retrieval,
evaluation and cross-system coordination, whereas bias altered the routing of socially salient information into
retrieval and normative-control pathways. Because these multilevel signatures were predictive and steerable
under parcel intervention, NeuroCogMap links systems-level interpretation to mechanism-guided diagnosis
and intervention.
Sycophancy reflects distributed weakening of independent judgment
We next examined sycophancy as a second behavioural-control pathology, focusing first on the Feedback setting
of Sycophancy-Eval by comparing independent and sycophantic responses. In contrast to refusal failure, which
reflected a shift into procedural action generation, sycophancy was characterized by a broader attenuation of
independent evaluative processing. Across circuit, parcel, capability and hierarchy levels, independent responses
showed stronger recruitment of normative reasoning, contradiction-sensitive control and self-regulatory decision
mechanisms, whereas sycophantic responses showed greater reliance on socially compliant or externally guided
processing (Extended Data Fig. 9a–d).
At the circuit level, independent responses were supported by a control-oriented network linking moral
evaluation, contradiction analysis and self-regulatory decision processes. Legal-Moral Evaluation coupled more
strongly with the Decision-Initiation Module (Δ𝑤= 0.28, 𝑃= 4.2 × 10−15) and with Moral Lesson Detector
(Δ𝑤= 0.16, 𝑃= 8.7 × 10−21), while Moral Lesson Detector further coupled with Decision-Initiation Module
(Δ𝑤= 0.16, 𝑃= 2.4 × 10−24). Independent responses also showed stronger Causal Extraction–Contradiction
Analysis coupling (Δ𝑤= 0.15, 𝑃= 5.8 × 10−28), together with inhibitory control linking Legal-Moral Evaluation
and Decision-Initiation to Self-Refusal Detection (Δ𝑤= 0.17, 𝑃= 1.6 × 10−25; Δ𝑤= 0.13, 𝑃= 1.1 × 10−20). This
pattern suggests that independent responding is actively maintained by circuits that detect conflict, evaluate
normative implications and suppress premature agreement when correction is warranted.
Sycophantic responses, by contrast, strengthened pathways associated with obedience and self-referential
social cues, together with stronger inhibitory links involving contradiction-sensitive retrieval pathways. These
included Self-Pride Recognition–Harassment Detection Module (Δ𝑤= −0.26, 𝑃< 10−300), Narrative Media
Comprehension–Listening and Obedience Processing (Δ𝑤= −0.20, 𝑃< 10−300), and inhibitory links from
Contradiction Analysis to Factoid Retrieval (Δ𝑤= −0.33, 𝑃= 3.0 × 10−41) and from Geopolitical-Trivia Retrieval
to Numeric Extraction and Arithmetic (Δ𝑤= −0.31, 𝑃= 5.2 × 10−17). Thus, sycophancy did not primarily reflect

by AUROC in the Answer and Feedback settings in Gemma2-2B and Gemma2-9B-IT. f, Sycophancy mitigation by NeuroCogMap-guided

performed on Gemma2-2B. Error bars indicate 95% confidence intervals in b,c and standard deviation in e. Statistical significance in b,c,e
was assessed using two-sided paired 𝑡-tests: *𝑃< 0.05, **𝑃< 0.01, ***𝑃< 0.001; ns, not significant. Dataset details and sample sizes are
reported in Methods and Supplementary Table 1; intervention procedures are described in Methods.
stronger reasoning in favour of the user’s position, but a shift in control state in which contradiction-sensitive
retrieval was disrupted and socially receptive pathways gained influence (Extended Data Fig. 9a).
Parcel-level activity showed a consistent pattern. Independent responses more strongly activated Social
Norm Reasoning (Δ𝑎= 13.98, 𝑃= 4.2 × 10−32), Combinatorial Reasoning (Δ𝑎= 12.50, 𝑃= 0.0053), Legal-Moral
Evaluation (Δ𝑎= 4.51, 𝑃= 6.4×10−23), Modular Arithmetic Reasoner (Δ𝑎= 4.28, 𝑃= 0.0042) and Risk-Causality
Analysis (Δ𝑎= 3.93, 𝑃= 4.7 × 10−12). These parcels indicate that independence is not a passive absence of
agreement, but an actively maintained configuration involving social-norm evaluation, structured reasoning,
risk evaluation and moral judgment. Sycophantic responses instead showed stronger activation of Uncertainty
Clarification Module (Δ𝑎= −12.68, 𝑃= 1.0 × 10−71), Mathematical Reasoning Core (Δ𝑎= −7.47, 𝑃= 0.0020),
Logical Reasoning Core (Δ𝑎= −3.90, 𝑃= 1.9 × 10−60), Self-Pride Recognition (Δ𝑎= −2.57, 𝑃= 2.4 × 10−71)
and Application Knowledge Hub (Δ𝑎= −2.49, 𝑃= 2.9 × 10−8). The recruitment of Self-Pride Recognition is
particularly informative: sycophantic outputs appeared to increase sensitivity to user stance or self-image,
rather than strengthening modules that adjudicate whether the stance is correct. NeuroCogMap therefore
reveals sycophancy as a reweighting of internal processing towards interpersonal alignment at the expense of
independent evaluation (Extended Data Fig. 9b).
At the capability level, independent responses consistently showed stronger activation of Fairness (Δ𝑐= 2.81,
𝑃= 4.2 × 10−11), Ethical reasoning (Δ𝑐= 2.74, 𝑃= 1.9 × 10−7), Judgment (Δ𝑐= 1.44, 𝑃= 3.3 × 10−26),
Safety (Δ𝑐= 1.09, 𝑃= 1.2 × 10−4) and Verification (Δ𝑐= 1.04, 𝑃= 5.6 × 10−22). By contrast, sycophantic
responses were relatively stronger in Common sense reasoning (Δ𝑐= −0.61, 𝑃= 3.0 × 10−13) and Logical
reasoning (Δ𝑐= −0.38, 𝑃= 5.2 × 10−6). This dissociation suggests that sycophancy is unlikely to reflect a
simple loss of reasoning. Instead, socially independent judgment and verification are selectively weakened
while lower-level inferential competence remains available, producing responses that favour agreement over
correction (Extended Data Fig. 9c).
At the hierarchy level, independent responses were stronger across the four NeuroCogMap layers, with the
41


## Page 42


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
largest separation in Perceptual Access and Attentional Gating and Situated Application and Social Interaction.
This indicates that sycophancy is not confined to a single high-level decision module. Rather, it reflects a
distributed attenuation of independent processing, beginning with how socially relevant cues are attended to
and extending to how final socially situated responses are selected. The application-layer difference suggests
that the ultimate pathology lies in socially conditioned behavioural output, whereas the additional reduction at
lower levels suggests that user stance may already bias the initial framing of the interaction before explicit
evaluation is complete (Extended Data Fig. 9d).
Together, these findings distinguish sycophancy from refusal failure. Refusal failure reflects the release
of procedural execution when harmful instructions escape control, whereas sycophancy reflects a distributed
weakening of independent evaluative control in favour of socially aligned response selection. NeuroCogMap
therefore characterizes sycophancy as a behavioural-control pathology in which conflict monitoring, normative
evaluation and self-corrective judgment fail to maintain epistemic independence.
Mechanism-guided detection and mitigation of sycophancy
Finally, we asked whether the sycophancy-related signatures identified by NeuroCogMap could support diagnosis
and intervention. Using the same mechanism-guided framework, we constructed a detector from NeuroCogMap-
derived pathology signatures and evaluated it in the Answer and Feedback settings (Extended Data Fig. 9e). On
Gemma2-2B, NeuroCogMap achieved AUROC values of 0.685 for Answer and 0.646 for Feedback, exceeding
Perplexity (0.571 and 0.543), Self-Consistency (0.651 and 0.539) and User-Conditioned Attention (0.647
and 0.582). On Gemma2-9B-IT, NeuroCogMap again performed best overall, with AUROC values of 0.617 for
Answer and 0.696 for Feedback, compared with Perplexity (0.595 and 0.523), Self-Consistency (0.479 and
0.560) and User-Conditioned Attention (0.560 and 0.649). These results indicate that sycophancy is better
detected from distributed control abnormalities than from generic likelihood, sampled-response consistency or
local attention to user-conditioning cues alone.
We then derived steering policies from the sycophancy pathology map and tested whether the corresponding
signatures contributed to socially compliant response formation. NeuroCogMap-guided intervention reduced
the sycophancy rate in both models, with lower values indicating better mitigation (Extended Data Fig. 9f). On
Gemma2-2B, the sycophancy rate decreased from 28.4% to 26.4% in the Answer setting and from 59.1% to
57.3% in the Feedback setting. On Gemma2-9B-IT, the corresponding reductions were larger, from 20.2% to
18.0% for Answer and from 63.2% to 60.9% for Feedback. Thus, unlike refusal failure, where intervention
primarily restored a specific refusal-control pathway, sycophancy showed a more modest but consistent reduction,
consistent with the broader and more distributed weakening of independent control revealed by the preceding
analyses.
Together, the refusal-failure and sycophancy analyses indicate that behavioural-control pathologies in
LLMs are structured failures of output regulation rather than losses of general competence. Refusal failure
released procedural action-generation pathways when harmful instructions should have been inhibited, whereas
sycophancy weakened conflict monitoring, normative evaluation and self-corrective judgment under social
pressure. Because these control signatures were predictive and actionable under parcel steering, NeuroCogMap
links systems-level interpretation to mechanism-guided behavioural control.
Pathology datasets and representative task patterns
The main and extended pathology analyses used four pathology contrasts. Hallucination and social bias
were treated as representational pathologies, because the pathological response depends on distorted factual,
evidential or demographic representations. Refusal failure and sycophancy were treated as behavioural-control
pathologies, because the pathological response reflects a failure to regulate output under harmful instructions,
safety constraints or social pressure. The datasets were selected to separate superficially similar failures into
mechanistically distinct regimes.
Hallucination datasets.
TruthfulQA and NQ-Open were used as complementary hallucination probes. Truth-
fulQA tests whether a model can suppress salient but false misconception-based answers, whereas NQ-Open
tests whether a model can coordinate factual retrieval and answer selection for direct open-domain questions.

HaluEval provided a broader detection benchmark, MedHallu tested biomedical factuality, Dolly closed-QA
tested context-grounded evidence use and SciQ tested compact science-question retrieval. Representative task
patterns include misconception questions that require rejecting a familiar false belief, direct factual questions
such as date or authorship retrieval, context-based questions whose answer is present in a supplied passage
and biomedical questions in which hallucination may arise from mechanism misattribution.
Social-bias datasets.
The BBQ subsets contain short social contexts with answer options that typically include
two identity-linked options and an uncertainty option. A fair response often requires recognizing that the
context does not justify a demographic inference. The disability-status subset was emphasized for mechanistic
analysis because it directly tests whether disability-related category information is routed into cognitive, social
or occupational role inference. Representative task patterns include underdetermined contexts involving age,
disability status, gender identity or nationality, where a biased response selects the stereotype-aligned option
and a fair response selects uncertainty or another non-stereotyped answer.
Refusal-failure datasets.
AdvBench and JBB-Behaviors probe refusal failure as a breakdown of safety and
action gating under harmful instructions. AdvBench provides a compact set of direct harmful requests, whereas
JBB-Behaviors tests broader harmful behaviours and more contextualized requests. Representative examples
are paraphrased for safety: a prompt may request cyber abuse, deception, misinformation or other dangerous
instruction following. A successful refusal maintains a safety boundary and avoids operational guidance; refusal
failure provides helpful harmful content.
Sycophancy datasets.
Sycophancy-Eval Answer tests whether factual judgment is preserved under user
pressure. A neutral factual prompt is paired with user-suggestive variants, and a response is sycophantic
when it shifts from an initially correct answer to the user’s incorrect suggestion. Sycophancy-Eval Feedback
43


## Page 44


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
tests whether evaluative judgments shift in a user-flattering direction for arguments, poems or mathematical
solutions. Representative task patterns include a user expressing doubt about the correct answer, suggesting an
incorrect answer or indicating that they personally wrote or liked an item being critiqued.
Pathology detection baselines
This section describes the baseline detectors used to contextualize NeuroCogMap-based pathology detection
in the hallucination, refusal-failure, sycophancy and social-bias analyses. The baselines were chosen to cover
three broad comparison families: output-distribution uncertainty, response-consistency measures and generic
internal-state or attention probes. Unless otherwise stated, baseline detectors were evaluated on the same
normative versus pathological labels used by NeuroCogMap and using the same per-dataset, per-model stratified
fivefold cross-validation splits.
Shared evaluation protocol
For each pathology family, the detection task was formulated as binary classification between a normative
condition and a pathological condition. Hallucination detection separated truthful or reference-supported
responses from hallucinated or unsupported responses. Refusal-failure detection separated successful refusal
from harmful compliance. Sycophancy detection separated independent responses from user-influenced
sycophantic responses. Social-bias detection separated unbiased or fair responses from biased responses.
Baselines that produced scalar scores, such as entropy, perplexity or consistency scores, were evaluated
as continuous detectors using held-out AUROC. Baselines that produced feature vectors, such as attention
features, hidden-state features or early-logit features, used a lightweight classifier fit within each training fold
and were evaluated from held-out predicted probabilities. This protocol ensured that differences between
NeuroCogMap and the baselines reflected the information source used for detection rather than differences in
labels or evaluation splits.
Hallucination detection baselines
We compared NeuroCogMap with six hallucination-detection baselines spanning token-level uncertainty,
sequence likelihood, semantic uncertainty, self-consistency and representation-level probing. These baselines
test whether hallucination can be detected from output probability distributions, sampled response variability
or generic hidden representations, without using the structured parcel, capability and circuit-level organization
provided by NeuroCogMap. This comparison follows prior work treating hallucination detection as a problem
of uncertainty estimation, selective generation, self-consistency assessment and hidden-state separability [68,
35, 36, 37, 33, 34].
Entropy.
Entropy is a token-level uncertainty baseline [35]. For each generated token, we computed the
entropy of the model’s next-token distribution,

where 𝑥is the prompt, 𝑦<𝑡is the generated prefix and V is the vocabulary. The response-level uncertainty
score was the aggregate token entropy across the generated answer,

Higher entropy indicates that the model distributes probability mass more broadly over possible next tokens
across the response. This provides a simple white-box uncertainty baseline, but it is affected by answer length
and does not directly test whether the generated content is factually correct.

LN Entropy.
LN Entropy (length-normalized entropy) controls for the dependence of aggregate uncertainty
on answer length [35]. For a response of 𝑇generated tokens, the baseline used average token entropy,

This value normalizes aggregate token entropy by response length and by the maximum possible vocabulary
entropy, log |V|. LN Entropy therefore measures bounded average predictive uncertainty per generated token,
rather than total uncertainty accumulated over the full answer. It remains a surface-level uncertainty measure
and can miss high-confidence hallucinations.
Perplexity.
Perplexity measures the likelihood assigned by the evaluated model to the generated response [36].
For each prompt-response pair, we computed the average negative log-likelihood of the generated answer
tokens and exponentiated it,

Higher perplexity indicates that the generated sequence is less probable under the model. In hallucination
detection, this baseline tests whether unsupported answers are also lower-likelihood generations. Its limitation
is that fluent hallucinations can remain highly probable when they follow common linguistic or factual patterns.
Semantic Entropy.
Semantic Entropy estimates uncertainty at the level of meaning rather than individual
tokens [37]. Multiple responses were sampled for the same prompt and grouped into semantic equivalence
classes. The semantic entropy score was computed over the empirical distribution of semantic clusters,
𝐻sem(𝑥) = −
∑︁
𝑐∈C
𝑝(𝑐| 𝑥) log 𝑝(𝑐| 𝑥),
where C denotes the set of semantic clusters. A high score indicates that repeated generations produce
semantically diverse or mutually inconsistent answers. This baseline is stronger than token entropy when
paraphrases express the same answer, but it requires multiple generations and semantic clustering for each
prompt.
SelfCheckGPT.
SelfCheckGPT is a black-box self-consistency baseline [33]. For each prompt, multiple
sampled responses were generated from the same model and compared with the original response. The
core assumption is that factual content supported by the model’s internal knowledge should be reproduced
consistently across samples, whereas hallucinated content is more likely to vary or contradict alternative
generations. The hallucination score was derived from inconsistency between the original response and the
sampled responses. SelfCheckGPT does not require logits or hidden states, but it is generation-expensive and
can fail when a model consistently repeats the same incorrect answer.
Hidden Probing.
Hidden Probing tests whether hallucination labels are decodable from generic internal
activations [34]. For each prompt-response pair, hidden states from selected model layers were aggregated into
a response-level representation, such as an answer-token average or final-token representation. A lightweight
classifier was then trained to predict the hallucination label,
ˆ𝑧= 𝑔(ℎ),
where ℎdenotes the extracted hidden representation and ˆ𝑧is the predicted probability of hallucination. This
baseline uses internal model information rather than output probabilities, but it does not identify which
functional parcels, capabilities or circuit-level relations contribute to the pathological state.

Refusal-failure detection baselines
Refusal-failure baselines were selected to test whether harmful compliance can be detected from prompt
likelihood, early output distributions or perturbation sensitivity. The normative class comprised successful
refusal, whereas the pathological class comprised refusal failure or jailbreak success. These baselines are related
to prior work on adversarial prompting and jailbreak defense in aligned language models [70, 41, 42, 40].
Logits SVM.
Logits SVM follows a single-pass jailbreak-detection strategy [41]. For each input prompt, a
single forward pass was used to extract the next-token probabilities associated with the first few output-token
positions. If 𝑞𝑡,𝑣= 𝑝(𝑣| 𝑥, 𝑦<𝑡) denotes the probability assigned to vocabulary item 𝑣at early output position 𝑡,

where ˆ𝑟= 1 denotes predicted refusal failure. The rationale is that unsafe or jailbreak-inducing prompts may
alter the model’s earliest generation distribution before the full harmful answer is produced. This baseline is
computationally efficient because it does not require repeated generations, but it treats refusal failure as a
separability problem in the early output distribution rather than as a structured breakdown of internal control
organization.

SmoothLLM is a perturbation-robustness baseline for adversarial prompts [42]. For each

Benign prompts are expected to produce relatively stable outputs under small perturbations, whereas adversarial
prompts are expected to be less stable. SmoothLLM tests a different source of evidence from NeuroCogMap:
it detects prompt sensitivity rather than the internal configuration of safety, refusal and action-generation
mechanisms.
Perplexity.
Perplexity was used as a likelihood-based refusal-failure baseline [40]. For a prompt or prompt-
response sequence 𝑠= (𝑠1, . . . , 𝑠𝑇), the baseline computed

High-perplexity examples were treated as more likely to be adversarial or pathological. This baseline is
motivated by the observation that some jailbreak prompts are distributionally unusual or unnatural. However,
perplexity can miss fluent harmful instructions and can also assign high scores to benign but rare prompts.
Sycophancy detection baselines
Sycophancy is a comparatively recent detection setting, and there is less consensus on standard detectors than
in hallucination or jailbreak detection. We therefore constructed baselines from existing sycophancy analyses
and related consistency-based detection methods [71, 96, 92, 33]. The normative class comprised independent
responses, whereas the pathological class comprised responses that shifted toward the user’s stated belief,
preference or self-presentation.

Self-Consistency.
Self-Consistency tests whether the model maintains a stable answer when user-pressure
information is not treated as decisive evidence [33, 96]. For each sycophancy item, repeated sampled responses
𝑦(1), . . . , 𝑦(𝑀) were compared under the same user-conditioned prompt. Outputs were grouped according to
semantic similarity, producing clusters C. The self-consistency score was summarized as

Low consistency or high semantic dispersion indicates that the model’s answer is unstable under the user-
conditioned setting. This baseline adapts the self-consistency intuition used in hallucination detection to
sycophancy, where the relevant failure is not factual unsupportedness alone, but susceptibility to user-guided
response shifts.
User-Conditioned Attention.
User-Conditioned Attention tests whether sycophancy is associated with ex-
cessive attention to user-belief or user-opinion spans [92]. For each prompt, user-conditioning cues were
identified, such as statements indicating that the user believes an answer, likes an item, dislikes an item, wrote
an item or suggests an alternative answer. If 𝑈denotes cue-token positions and 𝐴ℓ,𝑡,𝑢denotes attention from

legitimate evidence-based answer revision.
Social-bias detection baselines
Social-bias baselines were selected to test whether biased responses can be detected from demographic-attention
patterns, generic hidden-state separability or likelihood asymmetries among stereotype-relevant answer options.
The normative class comprised unbiased, fair or uncertainty-preserving responses, whereas the pathological
class comprised biased responses. This comparison is motivated by prior work measuring stereotypical bias
and fairness failures in language models [97, 69, 98, 99].
47

whether the response was biased. This baseline tests whether social bias is locally reflected in attention allocation
to demographic information, but attention features alone do not establish how demographic information interacts
with downstream reasoning or answer selection.
Hidden Probing.
Hidden Probing uses generic model hidden states to detect social-bias labels [99]. For each
prompt-response pair, hidden states from selected layers were aggregated into response-level representations,
with middle and late layer features retained to capture both intermediate and output-proximal information. If
ℎmid and ℎlate denote the aggregated middle- and late-layer states, the probe used
ℎ= [ℎmid; ℎlate],

where ˆ𝑏is the predicted probability of a biased response. This baseline asks whether the bias label is decodable
from internal states, but it does not explain which functional parcels or capabilities are responsible for the
biased response.
Perplexity.
Perplexity was used as a likelihood-based social-bias baseline [97]. For each multiple-choice
social-bias item, uncertainty or abstention options were removed when necessary, leaving the substantive
stereotype-relevant alternatives 𝑜1 and 𝑜2. This removal was used only to construct likelihood-asymmetry
features between stereotype-relevant alternatives; response labels and AUROC evaluation retained the original
fair or unbiased versus biased labels, including uncertainty-preserving responses. The model likelihood was
computed for each substantive answer option and for the model’s actual answer 𝑦under the question context

2{PPL(𝑜1 | 𝑥) + PPL(𝑜2 | 𝑥)}. This baseline tests whether biased responses are accompanied by
likelihood asymmetries among stereotype-relevant alternatives, but it does not identify the representational
mechanism by which demographic information influences inference.
Comparison with NeuroCogMap
The baselines above provide complementary but relatively coarse evidence about pathological responses.
Entropy, LN Entropy and Perplexity quantify uncertainty or likelihood in the model’s output distribution.
Semantic Entropy, SelfCheckGPT, SmoothLLM and Self-Consistency quantify instability across sampled or
perturbed responses. Logits SVM, Attention Probing, User-Conditioned Attention and Hidden Probing use early
logits, attention features or generic hidden states to test whether pathology labels are decodable from local or
unstructured internal signals.
NeuroCogMap differs from these baselines by representing each response through structured deviations
in parcel activation, capability recruitment and circuit connectivity. It therefore uses the same multilevel
cognitive organization that supports interpretation to construct the detector. This makes NeuroCogMap a
stronger mechanistic comparison point: it tests whether pathology is better detected as a coordinated disruption
of internal cognitive organization than as a generic change in uncertainty, likelihood, response instability,
attention to salient tokens or hidden-state separability.

(c). d–g, NeuroCogMap-guided parcel intervention in Llama-3.1-8B across hallucination (d), social bias (e), refusal failure (f) and
sycophancy (g). Hallucination is measured by answer accuracy on MedHallu, NQ-Open and TruthfulQA; social bias by fairness accuracy,
defined as the unbiased-response rate on BBQ subsets; refusal failure by refusal accuracy on JBB-Behaviors and AdvBench; and sycophancy
by sycophancy rate, where lower values indicate improvement. Social-bias detection results are not plotted because AUROC values were
close to ceiling across BBQ subsets. Bars show cross-validation means for detection and dataset-level outcomes for intervention; error bars
indicate standard deviation where available.
Cross-model safety-pathology detection and intervention in Llama-3.1-8B
To test whether NeuroCogMap-derived pathology signatures were restricted to the Gemma2 model family, we
repeated the safety-pathology detection and intervention analyses in Llama-3.1-8B [25]. The analysis used the
same normative-versus-pathological labels, pathology families and baseline definitions as the main Gemma2
experiments. Detection was evaluated by AUROC, and intervention was evaluated by the task-level behavioural
outcome used for each pathology family (Extended Data Fig. 10).
In hallucination detection, NeuroCogMap showed the strongest AUROC among the plotted methods in Llama-
3.1-8B on both datasets. On NQ-Open, NeuroCogMap achieved an AUROC of 0.691, exceeding SelfCheckGPT
(0.673), Perplexity (0.638), Hidden Probing (0.632), Entropy (0.625), LN Entropy (0.586), and Semantic
Entropy (0.580). On TruthfulQA, NeuroCogMap also ranked highest, with an AUROC of 0.663, outperforming
Hidden Probing (0.632), Perplexity (0.624), Entropy (0.570), Semantic Entropy (0.549), LN Entropy (0.548),
and SelfCheckGPT (0.496). Thus, hallucination detection in Llama-3.1-8B showed a consistent NeuroCogMap
signature across both open-domain factual answering and truthfulness-oriented evaluation, with the largest
margin observed on TruthfulQA.
Refusal-failure detection showed stronger cross-model generalization. NeuroCogMap achieved AUROC
values of 0.984 on AdvBench and 0.953 on JBB-Behaviors, exceeding Perplexity (0.852 and 0.842), Logits
SVM (0.486 and 0.570) and SmoothLLM (0.485 and 0.521). These results indicate that refusal failure in
Llama-3.1-8B was more clearly detected from structured NeuroCogMap signatures than from prompt likelihood,
early-output logits or perturbation sensitivity.
Sycophancy detection was more modest. NeuroCogMap reached AUROC values of 0.642 on the Answer track
and 0.660 on the Feedback track. These values exceeded Perplexity (0.567 and 0.539) and Self-Consistency
(0.519 and 0.402), and were comparable to User-Conditioned Attention (0.675 and 0.657). This pattern
suggests that sycophancy in Llama-3.1-8B is partially reflected in structured NeuroCogMap pathology signatures,
but also remains decodable from user-conditioned local signals.
Social-bias detection was not included in the plotted detection panel because all methods showed limited
discriminative headroom under near-ceiling separability across BBQ subsets. NeuroCogMap reached AUROC
values of 1.00 on BBQ-Age, BBQ-Nationality, BBQ-Gender and BBQ-Disability. This may reflect highly sep-
49


## Page 50


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
arable response-state structure in these BBQ subsets for Llama-3.1-8B; we therefore report these values as
supplementary evidence rather than using them as a main visual comparison.
NeuroCogMap-guided parcel intervention produced directionally consistent improvements across most
Llama-3.1-8B pathology settings. For hallucination, answer accuracy increased from 78.6% to 84.7% on
MedHallu, from 42.9% to 46.1% on NQ-Open and from 26.9% to 29.9% on TruthfulQA. For social bias,
intervention produced heterogeneous effects, with a small decrease on BBQ-Age (69.2% to 68.8%), minimal
change on BBQ-Nationality (71.5% to 71.6%), and improvements on BBQ-Gender (70.6% to 72.8%) and
BBQ-Disability (50.1% to 54.3%). For refusal failure, refusal accuracy increased from 23.2% to 30.2% on
JBB-Behaviors and from 72.4% to 79.8% on AdvBench. For sycophancy, the sycophancy rate decreased from
26.4% to 25.3% on the Answer track and from 64.4% to 61.9% on the Feedback track.
Together, these Llama-3.1-8B analyses indicate that NeuroCogMap-derived pathology signatures are not
restricted to the Gemma2 model family. The strongest cross-model generalization was observed for refusal-
failure detection and hallucination/refusal intervention, whereas social-bias detection showed near-ceiling
separability and sycophancy intervention produced smaller but directionally consistent changes.
Cross-model NeuroCogMap atlas and parcel–capability mapping summaries
Parcel construction is stable across clustering hyperparameters
We examined whether the NeuroCogMap parcellation was sensitive to clustering hyperparameters by repeating
parcel construction on Gemma2-2B across 16 settings defined by four SVD variance-retention thresholds (75%,
80%, 85% and 90%) and four numbers of clustering iterations (30, 50, 70 and 100). Similarity between two
parcellation solutions was quantified using the symmetric best-match Jaccard score, which accounts for the
fact that parcel labels are not naturally aligned across separately constructed parcellation solutions.
Across all off-diagonal pairs of parcellation solutions, parcel assignments were highly consistent (mean
similarity, 0.786; median similarity, 0.725; range, 0.642–1.000; Supplementary Table 4). This stability was
especially strong when the SVD variance-retention threshold was fixed and only the number of clustering
iterations varied. Within-threshold similarities were nearly identical across iteration settings (mean similarity,
0.999; median similarity, 1.000; range, 0.995–1.000), indicating that the clustering procedure had effectively
converged within the tested iteration range.
By contrast, variation in the SVD variance-retention threshold produced a structured, rather than random,
change in parcel composition. The lowest similarities occurred when the 75% retention setting was compared
with higher-retention solutions (75% versus 80%: mean similarity, 0.663; 75% versus 85%: 0.642; 75% versus
90%: 0.642), indicating that aggressive dimensionality reduction altered part of the parcel structure. Similarity
increased when more latent variance was retained: the 80% solution showed stronger agreement with the
85% and 90% solutions (both mean similarity, 0.725), whereas the 85% and 90% solutions were almost
indistinguishable (mean similarity, 1.000; range, 0.999–1.000). Thus, parcel construction was insensitive to the
number of optimization iterations and converged to highly stable assignments once sufficient latent variance
was retained.
To assess whether these similarities exceeded chance, we compared each observed solution pair against
a size-preserving permutation null in which latent assignments were randomly reassigned while keeping
parcel sizes fixed. All off-diagonal solution pairs were significantly more similar than the corresponding
null distribution (all one-sided permutation tests, 𝑃≤0.001, 1,000 permutations). Together, these results
indicate that NeuroCogMap parcel construction is robust to clustering-iteration settings and shows a stable
high-retention regime across SVD thresholds, supporting the reproducibility of the functional atlas.
Capability-associated parcels show hierarchical semantic and structural organization
Fig. 2d shows that the NeuroCogMap hierarchy is supported by both semantic coherence among hierarchy-
linked parcels and functional dependency from lower-level support processes to higher-level reasoning. Related
work in cognitive and network neuroscience has examined hierarchical organization in terms of both functional
diversity and the structural position of components within a broader system. Motivated by this perspective,
we characterized the parcels associated with each NeuroCogMap hierarchy level along two complementary

dimensions: semantic dispersion among associated parcel functions and graph-theoretic degree within the
model-specific structural connectome. These analyses were performed on the Gemma2-2B NeuroCogMap atlas
and repeated in the Llama-3.1-8B atlas as an independent cross-model replication.
First, we quantified the semantic dispersion of parcels associated with each cognitive level (Extended
Data Fig. 11a,b). For each capability, we selected its highest-ranked associated parcels and represented each
parcel by the concatenation of its functional name and description. Parcel descriptions were embedded with
Qwen3-Embedding-8B, and semantic dispersion was defined as one minus the mean pairwise cosine similarity
among parcel descriptions within a hierarchy level. To assess robustness to the number of parcels retained per
capability, the analysis was repeated after retaining the top 5, 10, 15, 20 or 25 parcels. Semantic dispersion
increased along the cognitive hierarchy in Gemma2-2B, from perceptual access and attentional gating to
situated application and social interaction (0.406 ± 0.011 to 0.466 ± 0.007; linear trend 𝑅2 = 0.877). The
same overall pattern was observed in Llama-3.1-8B (0.400 ± 0.003 to 0.454 ± 0.004; 𝑅2 = 0.857). Thus,
higher-level capabilities were associated with parcel sets that were less mutually similar and occupied a broader
functional space, consistent with the idea that abstraction and situated application require the coordination of
more heterogeneous component operations.
Second, we examined whether hierarchy-linked parcels differed in their structural position based on the
model-specific structural connectome (Extended Data Fig. 11c,d). For graph-theoretic summarization, the

Similarity was computed as the symmetric best-match Jaccard score [100] between two parcellation solutions. The 16 solutions were
generated from four SVD variance-retention thresholds (75%, 80%, 85% and 90%) and four clustering-iteration settings (30, 50, 70
and 100). For the permutation null, latent assignments were randomly reassigned while preserving parcel sizes. The reported 𝑃value

directed parcel–parcel connectivity matrix was converted into an undirected, unweighted graph by retaining the
strongest 10% of off-diagonal absolute weights; because the underlying connectome is directed, an undirected
edge was counted if either direction exceeded the threshold. Parcel degree was defined as the number of
neighbours in this thresholded graph. Each capability contributed its ten highest-ranked parcels; duplicate
parcels were collapsed within each hierarchy level and ranked by recurrence across capabilities, and robustness
was assessed by retaining 10, 15, 20, 25 or 30 representative parcels per level. Mean degree decreased across
the hierarchy in Gemma2-2B, from 62.71 ± 4.10 for perceptual access and attentional gating to 34.84 ± 3.40
for situated application and social interaction (𝑅2 = 0.921). The decrease was replicated in Llama-3.1-8B, from
66.64 ± 2.53 to 31.95 ± 1.47 (𝑅2 = 0.996). Lower-level capability-associated parcels therefore occupied more
connected positions than higher-level parcels within the model-level structural connectome.
Together, these analyses link the semantic and structural organization of hierarchy-associated parcels
(Extended Data Fig. 11). Higher levels were associated with broader parcel semantics, consistent with the
integration of multiple component operations, whereas lower levels occupied more connected positions in the
structural connectome, consistent with reusable access and representational support. This division of labour
complements the dependency results in Fig. 2d and suggests a hierarchical architecture in which functionally
diverse higher-order cognition is supported by a broadly connected lower-level substrate. The same organization
echoes a broader cognitive-neuroscience view in which complex cognition emerges through the coordinated
reuse of representational resources within structured networks.

contextual and parametric support. Each synthetic profile contained a name and five attributes, including
birth date, birth city, university, major and company. Two-hop examples were generated from pairs of profiles
sharing an attribute; only one source profile is shown here for compactness.

During final evaluation, the model received the available context when present, followed by the question
and Answer:. Generation used temperature 0, top-𝑝= 1 and maximum length 50, and a prediction was
counted as correct if the generated text contained the gold answer string.
Psych-101 cognitive-task datasets used in Fig. 6b
Fig. 6b tested whether NeuroCogMap activations predict variation in the behavioural fit between a language
model and individual human participants across distinct cognitive domains. We analysed five participant-level
datasets from Psych-101 [58], spanning episodic long-term memory, multi-attribute decision-making, Shepard
categorization, intertemporal choice and drifting four-armed bandit learning. As described in Methods, each
participant history was converted into a behavioural context, Gemma2-2B was evaluated on the likelihood
assigned to the observed human responses, and participant-level NeuroCogMap activation vectors were used to
predict held-out behavioural fit. The details below specify the task structure, participant counts and behavioural
sequences used in this analysis.
Episodic long-term memory.
The episodic long-term memory dataset was taken from the intentional versus
incidental learning experiment of Popov and Dames [62]. Participants completed repeated cycles containing a
word-study phase, an arithmetic distractor phase and a recall phase. During study, each word was presented
with a coloured border indicating whether it should be intentionally remembered, while participants also made
a semantic judgement about whether the object denoted by the word was larger or smaller than a football. The
analysed behavioural record therefore combined encoding decisions and later recall responses. Representative
items included words such as “church”, “robot” and “jewelry”; model fit reflected the likelihood assigned to
observed semantic judgements and recall outputs in the context of the preceding study history.

Multi-attribute choice.
This dataset was taken from the multi-attribute choice experiment of Hilbig and
Moshagen [63]. Participants repeatedly chose between two fictitious products. Each product was described by
four binary expert ratings, ordered from the most reliable to the least reliable cue. In a representative trial, two
products were assigned binary rating vectors such as [0 1 1 1] and [1 0 0 1], and the participant selected one
product. Model fit reflected the likelihood assigned to the observed choice given the full pattern of cue values
and cue validities.
Shepard categorization.
The Shepard categorization dataset was taken from the category-learning experi-
ment of Badham, Sanborn and Maylor [64]. Participants learned category rules for simple geometric objects
varying in features such as shape, size and colour. After each judgement, feedback indicated the correct category,
allowing learning to unfold over the sequence of examples. A representative event consisted of viewing an
object such as a large black square or a small black triangle, assigning it to one of two categories and receiving
the correct label. Model fit reflected the likelihood of the participant’s category judgement conditional on
previous examples and feedback within the current rule-learning problem.
Intertemporal choice.
The intertemporal choice dataset was taken from the cross-national temporal-discounting
study of Ruggeri et al. [65]. Participants chose between smaller immediate monetary outcomes and larger
delayed outcomes. Trials included both gains and losses, allowing the task to probe discounting over time and
sensitivity to monetary framing. Representative trials involved choosing between receiving a fixed amount
immediately and receiving a larger amount later, or between paying an amount immediately and paying a
larger amount later. Model fit reflected the probability assigned to the observed immediate or delayed choice
given the amount, delay and gain/loss frame.
Drifting four-armed bandit.
The drifting four-armed bandit dataset was taken from the four-arm bandit
dataset of Bahrami and Navajas [66]. Participants repeatedly chose among four options and received point
feedback after each choice. Because option values drifted over time, the task required participants to update
reward expectations while balancing exploitation of currently rewarding options with continued exploration.
The behavioural record consisted of a sequential history of selected arms and obtained rewards, with model
fit reflecting the likelihood assigned to each observed arm choice given the participant’s accumulated reward
history.
Human evaluation of LLM-assisted NeuroCogMap components
Experimental setting.
We conducted a targeted human audit of NeuroCogMap components that depended
on LLM-assisted annotation, automated judgement or cross-model functional matching. The goal of this audit
was not to relabel the full study, but to test whether the most judgement-dependent links in the pipeline were
supported by independent human assessment. Three annotators with undergraduate training in computer
science independently evaluated blinded annotation files. Annotators were instructed to fill only the prespecified
scoring columns and were not shown hidden automatic labels, LLM scores, embedding scores or matching
rationales during annotation. Categorical labels were summarized by majority vote, and ordinal scores were
summarized by the mean across annotators. Inter-annotator agreement was quantified using Fleiss’ 𝜅[101] for
55


## Page 56


NeuroCogMap Reveals Cognitive Organization of Large Language Models
categorical labels and two-way random-effects ICC(2,1) [102] for ordinal ratings.
The audit contained three components. First, in the parcel functional annotation audit, annotators evaluated
60 sampled Gemma2-2B parcels from the NeuroCogMap functional atlas. For each parcel, annotators saw the
LLM-generated function name, functional description, model-role description, keywords, dataset-distribution
summary and high-activation examples. They rated specificity, faithfulness to examples, coverage of the
dominant activation pattern and overreach on 1–5 scales, and made a final pass, partial, fail or unclear decision.
Second, in the pathology-label audit, annotators evaluated 570 model responses spanning hallucination,
social bias, refusal failure and sycophancy. The annotation items included the prompt or question, model
response and task-specific reference information, but did not include the automated condition labels. Human
labels were compared with the automated labels used in the contrastive NeuroCogMap pathology analyses. We
report both exact label agreement and a coarser normative-versus-pathological agreement. The coarse analysis
maps truthful, no-bias or uncertain, anti-bias, successful-refusal and independent labels to the normative
class, and maps unsupported or hallucinated, contradicted, biased, refusal-failure or harmful-compliance and
sycophantic labels to the pathological class. Intermediate or ambiguous human labels, including partially factual,
partial refusal, ambiguous and no-majority cases, were excluded from the coarse agreement denominator.
Third, in the cross-model parcel-matching audit, annotators evaluated 100 real matched parcel pairs
between Gemma2-2B and Gemma2-9B-IT sampled from the cross-model matching table. This audit was
designed to test whether the reported matched pairs were judged by humans to have similar functions, rather
than whether matched pairs could be separated from random negative controls. Annotators saw the source
and target parcel names, descriptions, model-role summaries, keywords and representative high-activation
examples, and rated functional similarity on a 0–3 scale. They also provided a shared-domain judgement and
a final identical, partial, different or unclear decision. Automated LLM, embedding and combined matching
scores were retained for post hoc comparison with human judgements.
Parcel functional annotation audit.
Human annotators rated the parcel-level cognitive atlas positively.
Across 60 sampled parcels, LLM-generated parcel descriptions were rated as specific (mean 4.24, 95% CI
4.11–4.38), faithful to high-activation examples (4.46, 95% CI 4.26–4.66) and covering the dominant activation
pattern (4.41, 95% CI 4.21–4.60). Overreach scores were low (1.71, 95% CI 1.54–1.87), indicating that
annotators generally did not perceive the descriptions as making unsupported claims. Inter-annotator reliability
for the ordinal ratings was moderate for specificity, faithfulness and coverage (ICC = 0.456–0.498), and lower
for overreach (ICC = 0.319), consistent with overreach being the most subjective rating dimension.
Final categorical decisions also supported the parcel descriptions. Majority vote classified 52 parcels as pass,
4 as partial, 2 as fail and 2 as no-majority, giving a pass-or-partial rate of 56/60 = 93.3% (95% CI 84.1–97.4%).
Fleiss’ 𝜅for the pass, partial, fail or unclear decision was low (0.194), largely because the marginal distribution
was strongly concentrated in the pass category. Overall, these results indicate that the LLM-generated cognitive
atlas descriptions were usually judged by humans to be specific, faithful and adequately supported by the
activation examples.
Pathology-label audit.
The pathology-label audit showed substantial agreement among annotators and
broad agreement between human and automated labels. Across all 570 responses, inter-annotator agreement
was substantial (Fleiss’ 𝜅= 0.783). Majority-human labels exactly matched the automated labels for 417/570
items (73.2%). After collapsing labels into normative versus pathological classes and excluding intermediate,
ambiguous or no-majority cases, human and automated labels agreed on 426/515 valid items (82.7%).
Agreement varied by pathology type, reflecting differences in how sharply each category can be opera-
tionalized. Refusal failure showed the strongest validation: exact human–automatic agreement was 137/150 =
91.3%, and coarse normative/pathological agreement was 137/146 = 93.8% (Fleiss’ 𝜅= 0.910). Hallucination
showed lower exact agreement (91/150 = 60.7%) because many automatic truthful or hallucinated responses
were judged as partially factual by humans, but the coarse normative/pathological agreement was high (97/103
= 94.2%; 𝜅= 0.526). Sycophancy showed moderate agreement, with exact agreement of 115/150 = 76.7%
and coarse agreement of 115/146 = 78.8% (𝜅= 0.464). Social bias showed high inter-annotator consistency
(𝜅= 0.779), indicating that human raters applied the bias rubric consistently. Agreement with automated
labels was lower when the audit retained fine-grained social-bias categories: exact agreement was 74/120

= 61.7%, and coarse agreement was 77/120 = 64.2%. This discrepancy was concentrated in the automatic
anti-bias category, for which annotators often preferred a biased rather than anti-bias label. Because the main
NeuroCogMap analyses used the binary biased versus unbiased distinction rather than anti-bias as a separate
evaluation class, this discrepancy mainly indicates that social-bias labels are more rubric-sensitive than the
other pathology labels, especially when raters distinguish non-stereotyped answers from uncertainty-preserving
responses.
Annotators reported high confidence overall. Across all pathology ratings, the mean confidence score was
4.65 on a 1–5 scale, and only 16/1,710 individual annotations (0.9%) were marked as needing adjudication.
These results support the use of the automated pathology labels most strongly for refusal failure and coarse
hallucination contrasts, and support the binary social-bias analyses when interpreted with the dataset-specific
BBQ rubric. They also indicate that finer-grained anti-bias distinctions should be treated as rubric-sensitive
rather than as a primary evaluation label.
Cross-model parcel-matching audit.
The matched-only cross-model audit provided positive human support
for the reported parcel correspondences between Gemma2-2B and Gemma2-9B-IT. Across 100 real matched
pairs, the mean human functional-similarity score was 2.30 on a 0–3 scale (95% CI 2.13–2.46), where 2
denotes partial similarity and 3 denotes high similarity or equivalence. Inter-annotator reliability for the ordinal
similarity score was high (ICC = 0.838), and categorical final decisions also showed substantial agreement
(Fleiss’ 𝜅= 0.657).
By majority vote, 53 pairs were judged identical, 24 partially similar, 21 different and 2 no-majority. Thus,
77/100 pairs were judged by humans as either identical or partially similar (77.0%, 95% CI 67.8–84.2%).
Using the ordinal ratings, 71/100 pairs had a mean human similarity score of at least 2, again indicating partial-
or-greater similarity. These results support the cross-model matching table as a functional correspondence
map for most sampled pairs, while identifying a minority of candidate matches that humans did not regard as
functionally aligned.
Human ratings were also strongly aligned with the continuous matching scores used by the pipeline. Mean
human similarity correlated with the LLM score (Spearman 𝜌= 0.842, 𝑃= 5.5 × 10−28), the embedding
score (𝜌= 0.789, 𝑃= 1.7 × 10−22) and the combined score (𝜌= 0.856, 𝑃= 8.0 × 10−30). Pairs originally
classified by the LLM as identical received higher human similarity scores than pairs classified as partially
similar (2.96 versus 1.97; AUROC = 0.850; one-sided Mann–Whitney 𝑃= 8.9 × 10−10). Exact agreement
between the majority-human final category and the LLM similarity type was 55/98 = 56.1% after excluding
two no-majority human cases. This exact category agreement is lower than the continuous-score agreement
because many LLM-partial pairs were judged by humans as either identical or different; nevertheless, the
strong ordinal correlations indicate that the original matching scores captured a human-recognizable gradient
of cross-model functional similarity.
